class="infobox-label">Education</th><td class="infobox-data"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1126788409"><div class="plainlist"><ul><li><a src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f0/Brian_Mulroney_Signature.svg/128px-Brian_Mulroney_Signature.svg.png" decoding="async" width="128" height="37" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f0/Brian_Mulroney_Signature.svg/192px-Brian_Mulroney_Signature.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f0/Brian_Mulroney_Signature.svg/256px-Brian_Mulroney_Signature.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="561" data-file-height="160" /></a></span></td></tr><tr><td colspan="2" class="infobox-below" style="border-top: 1px solid right;"><div></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Martin Brian Mulroney</b> <span class="noexcerpt nowraplinks" style="font-size:85%; font-weight:normal;"><a href="/wiki/King%27s_Privy_Council_for_Canada" title="King&#39;s Privy Council for Canada">PC</a>&#32;<a href="/wiki/Order_of_Canada" title="Order of Canada">CC</a>&#32;<a href="/wiki/National_Order_of_Quebec" title="National Order of Quebec">GOQ</a></span> (<span class="rt-commentedText nowrap"><span class="IPA nopopups noexcerpt" lang="en-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/English" title="Help:IPA/English">/<span style="border-bottom:1px dotted"><span title="&#39;m&#39; in &#39;my&#39;">m</span><span title="/ʊ/: &#39;u&#39; in &#39;push&#39;">ʊ</span><span title="&#39;l&#39; in &#39;lie&#39;">l</span><span title="/ˈ/: primary stress follows">ˈ</span><span title="&#39;r&#39; in &#39;rye&#39;">r</span><span title="/uː/: &#39;oo&#39; in &#39;goose&#39;">uː</span><span title="&#39;n&#39; in &#39;nigh&#39;">n</span><span title="/i/: &#39;y&#39; in &#39;happy&#39;">i</span></span>/</a></span></span> <a href="/wiki/Help:Pronunciation_respelling_key" title="Help:Pronunciation respelling key"><i title="English pronunciation respelling">muul-<span style="font-size:90%">ROO</span>-nee</i></a>; March 20, 1939 – February 29, 2024) was a Canadian lawyer, businessman, and politician who served as the 18th <a href="/wiki/Prime_minister_of_Canada" class="mw-redirect" title="Prime minister of Canada">prime minister of Canada</a> from 1984 to 1993.
</p><p>Born in the eastern Quebec city of <a href="/wiki/Baie-Comeau" title="Baie-Comeau">Baie-Comeau</a>, Mulroney studied <a href="/wiki/Political_science" title="Political science">political science</a> and law. He then moved to <a href="/wiki/Montreal" title="Montreal">Montreal</a> and gained prominence as a <a href="/wiki/Labour_law" title="Labour law">labour lawyer</a>. After placing third in the <a href="/wiki/1976_Progressive_Conservative_leadership_election" title="1976 Progressive Conservative leadership election">1976 Progressive Conservative leadership election</a>, he was appointed president of the <a href="/wiki/Iron_Ore_Company_of_Canada" title="Iron Ore Company of Canada">Iron Ore Company of Canada</a> in 1977. He held that post until <a href="/wiki/1983_Progressive_Conservative_leadership_election" title="1983 Progressive Conservative leadership election">1983</a>, when he became leader of the <a href="/wiki/Progressive_Conservative_Party_of_Canada" title="Progressive Conservative Party of Canada">Progressive Conservatives</a>. He led the party to a <a href="/wiki/Landslide_victory" title="Landslide victory">landslide victory</a> in the <a href="/wiki/1984_Canadian_federal_election" title="1984 Canadian federal election">1984 federal election</a>, winning the second-largest percentage of seats in Canadian history (at 74.8 percent) and receiving over 50 percent of the popular vote. He later won a second <a href="/wiki/Majority_government" title="Majority government">majority government</a> in <a href="/wiki/1988_Canadian_federal_election" title="1988 Canadian federal election">1988</a>.
</p><p>Mulroney's tenure as prime minister was marked by the introduction of major economic reforms, such as the <a href="/wiki/Canada%E2%80%93United_States_Free_Trade_Agreement" title="Canada–United States Free Trade Agreement">Canada–United States Free Trade Agreement</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Goods_and_services_tax_(Canada)" title="Goods and services tax (Canada)">goods and services tax</a> (GST) that was created to replace the manufacturers' sales tax, and the privatization of 23 of 61 <a href="/wiki/Crown_corporations_of_Canada" title="Crown corporations of Canada">Crown corporations</a> including <a href="/wiki/Air_Canada" title="Air Canada">Air Canada</a> and <a href="/wiki/Petro-Canada" title="Petro-Canada">Petro-Canada</a>. Mulroney sought Quebec's endorsement of the <a href="/wiki/Constitution_Act,_1982" title="Constitution Act, 1982">1982 constitutional amendments</a> by first introducing the <a href="/wiki/Meech_Lake_Accord" title="Meech Lake Accord">Meech Lake Accord</a> and then the <a href="/wiki/Charlottetown_Accord" title="Charlottetown Accord">Charlottetown Accord</a>. Both proposed recognizing Quebec as a <a href="/wiki/Distinct_society" title="Distinct society">distinct society</a>, extending <a href="/wiki/Canadian_provinces" class="mw-redirect" title="Canadian provinces">provincial</a> powers, and extensively changing the constitution. Both of the accords failed to be ratified, and the Meech Lake Accord's demise revived <a href="/wiki/Quebec_separatism" class="mw-redirect" title="Quebec separatism">Quebec separatism</a>, leading to the rise of the <a href="/wiki/Bloc_Qu%C3%A9b%C3%A9cois" title="Bloc Québécois">Bloc Québécois</a>. In foreign policy, Mulroney strengthened <a href="/wiki/Canada%E2%80%93United_States_relations" title="Canada–United States relations">Canada's ties with the United States</a> and opposed the <a href="/wiki/Apartheid" title="Apartheid">apartheid</a> regime in South Africa, leading an effort within the <a href="/wiki/Commonwealth_of_Nations" title="Commonwealth of Nations">Commonwealth</a> to sanction the country. Mulroney's tenure was marked by the <a href="/wiki/Air_India_Flight_182" title="Air India Flight 182">Air India Flight 182</a> bombing, the largest <a href="/wiki/Mass_killing" title="Mass killing">mass killing</a> in Canadian history, though his response to the attack came under criticism. Mulroney made <a href="/wiki/Environmental_protection" title="Environmental protection">environmental protection</a> a priority by securing <a href="/wiki/U.S.%E2%80%93Canada_Air_Quality_Agreement" title="U.S.–Canada Air Quality Agreement">a treaty with the United States on acid rain</a>, making Canada the first <a href="/wiki/Industrialized_country" class="mw-redirect" title="Industrialized country">industrialized country</a> to ratify the <a href="/wiki/Convention_on_Biological_Diversity" title="Convention on Biological Diversity">Convention on Biological Diversity</a>, adding significant national parks, and passing the <i><a href="/wiki/Canadian_Environmental_Assessment_Act" class="mw-redirect" title="Canadian Environmental Assessment Act">Environmental Assessment Act</a></i> and the <i><a href="/wiki/Canadian_Environmental_Protection_Act" class="mw-redirect" title="Canadian Environmental Protection Act">Environmental Protection Act</a></i>.
</p><p>The unpopularity of the GST and the controversy surrounding its passage in the <a href="/wiki/Senate_of_Canada" title="Senate of Canada">Senate</a>, combined with the <a href="/wiki/Early_1990s_recession" title="Early 1990s recession">early 1990s recession</a>, the collapse of the Charlottetown Accord, and growing <a href="/wiki/Western_alienation" title="Western alienation">Western alienation</a> that triggered the rise of the <a href="/wiki/Reform_Party_of_Canada" title="Reform Party of Canada">Reform Party</a>, caused a stark decline in Mulroney's popularity, which induced him to resign and <a href="/wiki/1993_Progressive_Conservative_leadership_election" title="1993 Progressive Conservative leadership election">hand over power</a> to his cabinet minister <a href="/wiki/Kim_Campbell" title="Kim Campbell">Kim Campbell</a> in June 1993. In <a href="/wiki/1993_Canadian_federal_election" title="1993 Canadian federal election">the election later that year</a>, the Progressive Conservatives were reduced from a majority government of 156 seats to two, with its support being eroded by the Bloc and Reform parties. In his retirement, Mulroney served as an international business consultant and sat on the board of directors of multiple corporations. Although he places average or above average in <a href="/wiki/Historical_rankings_of_prime_ministers_of_Canada" title="Historical rankings of prime ministers of Canada">rankings of Canadian prime ministers</a>, his legacy remains controversial. He was criticized for his role in the resurgence of <a href="/wiki/Quebec_nationalism" title="Quebec nationalism">Quebec nationalism</a> and accused of corruption in the <a href="/wiki/Airbus_affair" title="Airbus affair">Airbus affair</a>, a scandal that came to light only several years after he left office.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span id="Early_life_.281939.E2.80.931955.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life_(1939–1955)">Early life (1939–1955)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Brian_Mulroney&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Early life (1939–1955)"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Mulroney was born on March 20, 1939, in <a href="/wiki/Baie-Comeau" title="Baie-Comeau">Baie-Comeau</a>, Quebec, a remote and isolated town of the <a href="/wiki/C%C3%B4te-Nord" title="Côte-Nord">Côte-Nord</a> region, in the eastern part of the province. He was the son of <a href="/wiki/Irish_Canadian" class="mw-redirect" title="Irish Canadian">Irish Canadian</a> <a href="/wiki/Catholic" class="mw-redirect" title="Catholic">Catholic</a> parents, Mary Irene (née O'Shea) and Benedict Martin Mulroney,<sup id="cite_ref-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> who was a <a href="/wiki/Paper_mill" title="Paper mill">paper mill</a> electrician. As there was no English-language Catholic high school in Baie-Comeau, Mulroney completed his high school education at a <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic" class="mw-redirect" title="Roman Catholic">Roman Catholic</a> boarding school in <a href="/wiki/Chatham,_New_Brunswick" title="Chatham, New Brunswick">Chatham, New Brunswick</a>, operated by <a href="/wiki/St._Thomas_University_(Canada)" title="St. Thomas University (Canada)">St. Thomas University</a>. In 2001, St. Thomas University named its newest academic building in his honour. Benedict Mulroney worked overtime and ran a repair business to earn extra money for his children's education, and he encouraged his oldest son to attend university.<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceA_2-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceA-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Mulroney would frequently tell stories about newspaper publisher <a href="/wiki/Robert_R._McCormick" title="Robert R. McCormick">Robert R. McCormick</a>, whose company had founded Baie-Comeau. Mulroney would sing <a href="/wiki/Irish_music" class="mw-redirect" title="Irish music">Irish songs</a> for McCormick,<sup id="cite_ref-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> and the publisher would slip him $50.<sup id="cite_ref-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> Mulroney grew up speaking English and French fluently.<sup id="cite_ref-Donaldson310_5-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Donaldson310-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Family">Family</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Brian_Mulroney&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Family"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>On May 26, 1973, Mulroney married <a href="/wiki/Mila_Pivni%C4%8Dki" class="mw-redirect" title="Mila Pivnički">Mila Pivnički</a>, the daughter of a <a href="/wiki/Serbs" title="Serbs">Serbian</a>-Canadian doctor, <a href="/w/index.php?title=Dimitrije_Pivni%C4%8Dki&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Dimitrije Pivnički (page does not exist)">Dimitrije Pivnički</a><span class="noprint" style="font-size:85%; font-style: normal;">&#160;&#91;<a href="https://sr.wikipedia.org/wiki/%D0%94%D0%B8%D0%BC%D0%B8%D1%82%D1%80%D0%B8%D1%98%D0%B5_%D0%9F%D0%B8%D0%B2%D0%BD%D0%B8%D1%87%D0%BA%D0%B8" class="extiw" title="sr:Димитрије Пивнички">sr</a>&#93;</span>, from <a href="/wiki/Novi_Be%C4%8Dej" title="Novi Bečej">Novi Bečej</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Many PC campaign buttons featured both Mulroney's face and hers, and Ontario <a href="/wiki/Premier_of_Ontario" title="Premier of Ontario">Premier</a> <a href="/wiki/Bill_Davis" title="Bill Davis">Bill Davis</a> commented to Brian, "Mila will get you more votes for you than you will for yourself."<sup id="cite_ref-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Mulroneys have four children: <a href="/wiki/Caroline_Mulroney_Lapham" class="mw-redirect" title="Caroline Mulroney Lapham">Caroline</a>, <a href="/wiki/Ben_Mulroney" title="Ben Mulroney">Benedict (Ben)</a>, Mark and Nicolas. Caroline unsuccessfully ran for the <a href="/wiki/2018_Progressive_Conservative_Party_of_Ontario_leadership_election" title="2018 Progressive Conservative Party of Ontario leadership election">2018 Ontario PC leadership race</a> and represents the party in the provincial legislature as the member for <a href="/wiki/York%E2%80%94Simcoe_(provincial_electoral_district)" title="York—Simcoe (provincial electoral district)">York—Simcoe</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> She served as <a href="/wiki/Ontario%27s_minister_of_transportation" class="mw-redirect" title="Ontario&#39;s minister of transportation">Ontario's minister of transportation</a> and <a href="/wiki/Ministry_of_Francophone_Affairs" title="Ministry of Francophone Affairs">minister of Francophone affairs</a>. She moved from transportation to being <a href="/wiki/Treasury_Board_Secretariat_(Ontario)" title="Treasury Board Secretariat (Ontario)">President of the Treasury Board</a> while continuing on as the minister of Francophone affairs.<sup id="cite_ref-Ford_Cabinet_Shuffle_9-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ford_Cabinet_Shuffle-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> Ben was the host of the CTV morning show <i><a href="/wiki/Your_Morning" title="Your Morning">Your Morning</a></i> from June 2016 to October 2021.<sup id="cite_ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> Ben is married to stylist <a href="/wiki/Jessica_Mulroney" title="Jessica Mulroney">Jessica</a>, and their three children served as page boys and bridesmaids during the <a href="/wiki/Wedding_of_Prince_Harry_and_Meghan_Markle" title="Wedding of Prince Harry and Meghan Markle">wedding of Prince Harry and Meghan Markle</a> on May 19, 2018.<sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> Mark and Nicolas both work in the financial industry in Toronto.<sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Education_.281955.E2.80.931964.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Education_(1955–1964)">Education (1955–1964)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Brian_Mulroney&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Education (1955–1964)"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Mulroney entered <a href="/wiki/St._Francis_Xavier_University" title="St. Francis Xavier University">St. Francis Xavier University</a> in the fall of 1955 as a 16-year-old first-year student. His political life began when he was recruited to the campus Progressive Conservative group by <a href="/wiki/Lowell_Murray" title="Lowell Murray">Lowell Murray</a> and others early in his first year. Murray, who was appointed to the <a href="/wiki/Senate_of_Canada" title="Senate of Canada">Senate of Canada</a> in 1979, became Mulroney's close friend, mentor, and adviser. Mulroney made other important, lasting friendships with <a href="/wiki/Gerald_Doucet" title="Gerald Doucet">Gerald Doucet</a>, <a href="/wiki/Fred_Doucet" title="Fred Doucet">Fred Doucet</a>, <a href="/wiki/Sam_Wakim" title="Sam Wakim">Sam Wakim</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Patrick_MacAdam" title="Patrick MacAdam">Patrick MacAdam</a>. Mulroney enthusiastically embraced political organization and assisted the local PC candidate in his successful 1956 Nova Scotia provincial election campaign; the PCs, led provincially by <a href="/wiki/Robert_Stanfield" title="Robert Stanfield">Robert Stanfield</a>, won a surprise victory.<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceA_2-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceA-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Mulroney became a youth delegate and attended the 1956 leadership convention in <a href="/wiki/Ottawa" title="Ottawa">Ottawa</a>. While initially undecided, Mulroney was captivated by <a href="/wiki/John_Diefenbaker" title="John Diefenbaker">John Diefenbaker</a>'s powerful oratory and easy approachability. Mulroney joined the Youth for Diefenbaker committee, which was led by <a href="/wiki/Edward_Samuel_Rogers" class="mw-redirect" title="Edward Samuel Rogers">Ted Rogers</a>, a future <a href="/wiki/Kinship" title="Kinship">scion</a> of Canadian business. Mulroney struck an early friendship with Diefenbaker (who won the leadership) and received telephone calls from him.<sup id="cite_ref-Donaldson310_5-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Donaldson310-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Mulroney won several public speaking contests at St. Francis Xavier University, was a star member of the school's <a href="/wiki/Debating" class="mw-redirect" title="Debating">debating</a> team, and never lost an inter-university debate. He was also very active in campus politics, serving with distinction in several <a href="/wiki/Model_Parliament" title="Model Parliament">Model Parliaments</a>, and was campus prime minister in a <a href="/wiki/Maritimes" class="mw-redirect" title="Maritimes">Maritimes</a>-wide Model Parliament in 1958.<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceA_2-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceA-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Mulroney assisted with the 1958 national election campaign at the local level in Nova Scotia. This campaign led to the largest majority in the history of the Canadian House of Commons.<sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> After graduating from St. Francis Xavier with a degree in political science in 1959, Mulroney at first pursued a law degree from <a href="/wiki/Dalhousie_Law_School" class="mw-redirect" title="Dalhousie Law School">Dalhousie Law School</a> in <a href="/wiki/Halifax_Regional_Municipality" class="mw-redirect" title="Halifax Regional Municipality">Halifax</a>. It was around this time that Mulroney also cultivated friendships with the Tory <a href="/wiki/Premier_of_Nova_Scotia" title="Premier of Nova Scotia">premier of Nova Scotia</a>, <a href="/wiki/Robert_Stanfield" title="Robert Stanfield">Robert Stanfield</a>, and his chief adviser <a href="/wiki/Dalton_Camp" title="Dalton Camp">Dalton Camp</a>. In his role as an advance man, Mulroney assisted with Stanfield's successful 1960 re-election campaign. Mulroney neglected his studies, fell seriously ill during the winter term, was hospitalized, and, despite getting extensions for several courses because of his illness, left his program at Dalhousie after the first year.<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceA_2-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceA-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> He then applied to <a href="/wiki/Universit%C3%A9_Laval" title="Université Laval">Université Laval</a> in Quebec City and continued his legal studies there later in 1960.<sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In <a href="/wiki/Quebec_City" title="Quebec City">Quebec City</a>, Mulroney befriended future Quebec <a href="/wiki/Premier_of_Quebec" title="Premier of Quebec">Premier</a> <a href="/wiki/Daniel_Johnson_Sr." title="Daniel Johnson Sr.">Daniel Johnson Sr.</a> and frequented the provincial legislature, making connections with politicians, aides, and journalists. At Laval, Mulroney built a network of friends, including <a href="/wiki/Lucien_Bouchard" title="Lucien Bouchard">Lucien Bouchard</a>, <a href="/wiki/Bernard_Roy" title="Bernard Roy">Bernard Roy</a>, <a href="/wiki/Michel_Cogger" title="Michel Cogger">Michel Cogger</a>, <a href="/wiki/Michael_Meighen" title="Michael Meighen">Michael Meighen</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Jean_Bazin" title="Jean Bazin">Jean Bazin</a>, that would play a prominent role in Canadian politics for years to come.<sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Mulroney secured a temporary appointment in Ottawa during the summer of 1962 as the executive assistant to <a href="/wiki/Alvin_Hamilton" title="Alvin Hamilton">Alvin Hamilton</a>, minister of agriculture. Then, a federal election was called. Hamilton took Mulroney with him on the campaign trail, where the young organizer gained valuable experience.<sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Labour_lawyer_.281964.E2.80.931976.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Labour_lawyer_(1964–1976)">Labour lawyer (1964–1976)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Brian_Mulroney&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Labour lawyer (1964–1976)"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>After graduating from Laval in 1964, Mulroney moved to <a href="/wiki/Montreal" title="Montreal">Montreal</a> to join the law firm <a href="/wiki/Howard,_Cate,_Ogilvy_et_al." class="mw-redirect" title="Howard, Cate, Ogilvy et al.">Howard, Cate, Ogilvy et al.</a><sup id="cite_ref-:1_17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> The firm at the time was the largest law firm in the <a href="/wiki/Commonwealth_of_Nations" title="Commonwealth of Nations">Commonwealth of Nations</a>. Despite twice failing his bar exams, the firm kept him due to his charming personality.<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceA_2-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceA-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> Mulroney finally passed the exam and was admitted to the Quebec bar in 1965, after which he began practising as a labour lawyer.<sup id="cite_ref-:1_17-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> He worked on <a href="/wiki/Laurent_Picard" title="Laurent Picard">Laurent Picard</a>'s <a href="/wiki/Commission_of_Inquiry" class="mw-redirect" title="Commission of Inquiry">Commission of Inquiry</a> on the <a href="/wiki/Port_of_Montreal" title="Port of Montreal">St. Lawrence Ports</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-:1_17-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:1-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> He was noted for ending several strikes along the Montreal waterfront, where he met fellow lawyer <a href="/wiki/W._David_Angus" title="W. David Angus">W. David Angus</a> of <a href="/wiki/Stikeman_Elliott" title="Stikeman Elliott">Stikeman Elliott</a>, who would later become a valuable fundraiser for his campaigns.<sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup>  In addition, he met fellow then Stikeman Elliott lawyer <a href="/wiki/Stanley_Hartt" title="Stanley Hartt">Stanley Hartt</a>, who later played a vital role assisting him during his political career as Mulroney's Chief of Staff.<sup id="cite_ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1966, <a href="/wiki/Dalton_Camp" title="Dalton Camp">Dalton Camp</a>, who by then was president of the Progressive Conservative Party, ran for re-election in what many believed to be a referendum on Diefenbaker's leadership.<sup id="cite_ref-camp_20-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-camp-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> Diefenbaker had reached his 70th birthday in 1965. Mulroney joined with most of his generation in supporting Camp and opposing Diefenbaker, but due to his past friendship with Diefenbaker, he attempted to stay out of the spotlight. With Camp's narrow victory, Diefenbaker called for a <a href="/wiki/Progressive_Conservative_leadership_convention,_1967" class="mw-redirect" title="Progressive Conservative leadership convention, 1967">1967 leadership convention</a> in Toronto.<sup id="cite_ref-camp_20-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-camp-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> Mulroney joined with Joe Clark and others in supporting former Justice minister <a href="/wiki/E._Davie_Fulton" class="mw-redirect" title="E. Davie Fulton">E. Davie Fulton</a>. Once Fulton dropped off the ballot, Mulroney helped in swinging most of his organization over to <a href="/wiki/Robert_Stanfield" title="Robert Stanfield">Robert Stanfield</a>, who won. Mulroney, then 28, would soon become a chief adviser to the new leader in Quebec.<sup id="cite_ref-camp_20-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-camp-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Mulroney's professional reputation was further enhanced when he ended a strike that was considered impossible to resolve at the Montreal newspaper <a href="/wiki/La_Presse_(Canadian_newspaper)" title="La Presse (Canadian newspaper)"><i>La Presse</i></a>. In doing so, Mulroney and the paper's owner, Canadian business mogul <a href="/wiki/Paul_Desmarais" title="Paul Desmarais">Paul Desmarais</a>, became friends. After his initial difficulties, Mulroney's reputation in his firm steadily increased, and he was made a partner in 1971.<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceA_2-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceA-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Mulroney's big break came during the <a href="/wiki/Robert_Cliche" title="Robert Cliche">Cliche Commission</a> in 1974,<sup id="cite_ref-Lotz_21-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Lotz-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> which was set up by Quebec premier <a href="/wiki/Robert_Bourassa" title="Robert Bourassa">Robert Bourassa</a> to investigate the situation at the <a href="/wiki/James_Bay_Project" title="James Bay Project">James Bay Project</a>, Canada's largest <a href="/wiki/Hydroelectric" class="mw-redirect" title="Hydroelectric">hydroelectric</a> project. Violence and dirty tactics had broken out as part of a union accreditation struggle. To ensure the commission was non-partisan, Bourassa, the Liberal premier, placed Robert Cliche, a former leader of the provincial <a href="/wiki/New_Democratic_Party_of_Quebec" title="New Democratic Party of Quebec">New Democratic Party</a> in charge. Cliche asked Mulroney, a Progressive Conservative and a former student of his, to join the commission. Mulroney asked <a href="/wiki/Lucien_Bouchard" title="Lucien Bouchard">Lucien Bouchard</a> to join as counsel. The committee's proceedings, which showed <a href="/wiki/Rizzuto_crime_family" title="Rizzuto crime family">Mafia</a> infiltration of the unions, made Mulroney well known in Quebec, as the hearings were extensively covered in the media.<sup id="cite_ref-Lotz_21-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Lotz-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> The Cliche Commission's report was largely adopted by the Bourassa government. A notable incident included the revelation that the controversy may have involved the office of the Premier of Quebec when it emerged that Paul Desrochers, Bourassa's special executive assistant, had met with the union boss <a href="/wiki/Andr%C3%A9_Desjardins" title="André Desjardins">André Desjardins</a>, known as the "King of Construction," to ask for his help with winning a by-election in exchange guaranteeing that only companies employing workers from his union would work on the James Bay project.<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> Although Bouchard favoured calling in Robert Bourassa as a witness, Mulroney refused, deeming it a violation of 'executive privilege.'<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceA_2-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceA-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup>  Mulroney and Bourassa would later cultivate a friendship that would turn out to be extremely beneficial when Mulroney ran for re-election in 1988.<sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="1976_Progressive_Conservative_leadership_election">1976 Progressive Conservative leadership election</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Brian_Mulroney&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: 1976 Progressive Conservative leadership election"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The Stanfield-led Progressive Conservatives lost the <a href="/wiki/1974_Canadian_federal_election" title="1974 Canadian federal election">1974 election</a> to the <a href="/wiki/Pierre_Trudeau" title="Pierre Trudeau">Pierre Trudeau</a>-led Liberals, leading to Stanfield's resignation as leader. Mulroney, despite never having run for elected office, entered the contest to replace him. Mulroney and provincial rival <a href="/wiki/Claude_Wagner" title="Claude Wagner">Claude Wagner</a> were both seen as potentially able to improve the party's standing in Quebec, which had supported the federal Liberals for decades. Mulroney had played the lead role in recruiting Wagner to the PC party a few years earlier, and the two wound up as rivals for Quebec delegates, most of whom were snared by Wagner, who even blocked Mulroney from becoming a voting delegate at the convention.<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceA_2-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceA-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> In the leadership race, Mulroney spent an estimated $500,000, far more than the other candidates, and earned himself the nickname 'Cadillac candidate.' At the <a href="/wiki/1976_Progressive_Conservative_leadership_convention" class="mw-redirect" title="1976 Progressive Conservative leadership convention">1976 leadership convention</a>, Mulroney placed second on the first ballot behind Wagner. His expensive campaign, slick image, lack of parliamentary experience, and vague policy positions did not endear him to many delegates, and he was unable to build upon his base support, being overtaken by eventual winner Joe Clark on the second ballot. Mulroney was the only one of the eleven leadership candidates who did not provide full financial disclosure on his campaign expenses, and his campaign finished deeply in debt.<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceA_2-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceA-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Business_leadership_.281976.E2.80.931983.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Business_leadership_(1976–1983)">Business leadership (1976–1983)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Brian_Mulroney&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Business leadership (1976–1983)"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Mulroney took the job of executive vice president of the <a href="/wiki/Iron_Ore_Company_of_Canada" title="Iron Ore Company of Canada">Iron Ore Company of Canada</a>, a joint subsidiary of three major U.S. steel corporations. Mulroney earned a salary well into the six-figure range. In 1977, he was appointed company president. Drawing upon his labour law experience, he instituted improved labour relations, and, with commodity prices on the rise, company profits soared during the next several years. In 1983, Mulroney successfully negotiated the closing of the <a href="/wiki/Schefferville" title="Schefferville">Schefferville</a> mine, winning a generous settlement for the affected workers.<sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> In the wake of his loss in the 1976 leadership race, Mulroney battled alcohol abuse and depression for several years; he credits his loyal wife Mila with helping him recover. In 1979, he permanently became a teetotaller. During his IOC term, he made liberal use of the company's executive jet, frequently flying business associates and friends on fishing trips.<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceA_2-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceA-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> Mulroney also maintained and expanded his extensive political networking among business leaders and conservatives across the country. As his business reputation grew, he was invited onto several corporate boards.<sup id="cite_ref-camp_20-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-camp-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Opposition_leader_.281983.E2.80.931984.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Opposition_leader_(1983–1984)">Opposition leader (1983–1984)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Brian_Mulroney&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Opposition leader (1983–1984)"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Joe Clark led the Progressive Conservative party to a minority government in the <a href="/wiki/1979_Canadian_federal_election" title="1979 Canadian federal election">1979 federal election</a>, which ended 16 years of continuous Liberal rule. The government fell after a successful no-confidence motion over his minority government's budget in December 1979. The PCs subsequently lost the federal election held two months later to Trudeau and the Liberals. Many Tories were also annoyed with Clark over his slowness in dispensing patronage appointments after he became prime minister in June 1979. By late 1982, Joe Clark's leadership of the Progressive Conservatives was being questioned in many party circles and among many Tory members of Parliament, despite his solid national lead over Prime Minister Pierre Trudeau in opinion polls.<sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Mulroney_1983.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2a/Mulroney_1983.jpg/275px-Mulroney_1983.jpg" decoding="async" width="275" height="413" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2a/Mulroney_1983.jpg/413px-Mulroney_1983.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2a/Mulroney_1983.jpg/550px-Mulroney_1983.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1500" data-file-height="2250" /></a><figcaption>Mulroney on the floor of the 1983 leadership convention</figcaption></figure>
<p>Mulroney, while publicly endorsing Clark at a press conference in 1982, organized behind the scenes to defeat him at the party's <a href="/wiki/Leadership_review" title="Leadership review">leadership review</a>. Clark's key Quebec organizer, Rodrigue Pageau, was, in fact, a double agent, working for Mulroney, undermining Clark's support.<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceA_2-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceA-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> When Clark received an endorsement by only 66.9 percent of delegates at the party convention in January 1983 in <a href="/wiki/Winnipeg" title="Winnipeg">Winnipeg</a>, he resigned and ran to regain his post at the <a href="/wiki/Progressive_Conservative_leadership_convention,_1983" class="mw-redirect" title="Progressive Conservative leadership convention, 1983">1983 leadership convention</a>. Despite still not being a member of Parliament, Mulroney ran against him, campaigning more shrewdly than he had done seven years before. Mulroney had been criticized in 1976 for lacking policy depth and substance, a weakness he addressed by making several major speeches across the country in the early 1980s, which were collected into a book, <i>Where I Stand</i>, published in 1983.<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceA_2-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceA-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Mulroney also avoided most of the flash of his earlier campaign, for which he had been criticized. Mulroney was elected party leader on June 11, 1983, beating Clark on the fourth ballot, attracting broad support from the many factions of the party and especially from representatives of his native Quebec. Pundits noted that a poll of delegates on the final ballot showed that Mulroney had won a bare majority of Clark's home province of Alberta and that Clark had won a bare majority in Mulroney's home province of Quebec.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMartin,_Gregg,_Perlin237_27-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMartin,_Gregg,_Perlin237-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> Mulroney's strong showing amongst Ontario delegates (65 percent to 35 percent) seemed to account for most of his margin of victory.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMartin,_Gregg,_Perlin237_27-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMartin,_Gregg,_Perlin237-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> A <i>New York Times</i> article from 1984 argued that Mulroney was elected from "the right-wing elements" within the party.<sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup>   <a href="/wiki/Tasha_Kheiriddin" title="Tasha Kheiriddin">Tasha Kheiriddin</a>, writing in <i>La Presse</i>, argued that "Brian Mulroney's injuries to Joe Clark in 1983 took more than 15 years to heal, as various factions continued to compete for leadership roles in the field and youth wings."<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Two months later, Mulroney entered Parliament as the MP for <a href="/wiki/Central_Nova" title="Central Nova">Central Nova</a> in Nova Scotia, winning a by-election in what was then considered a safe Tory seat after <a href="/wiki/Elmer_MacKay" title="Elmer MacKay">Elmer MacKay</a> stood aside in his favour. The Progressive Conservatives only had one seat in Mulroney's home province of Quebec at the time.<sup id="cite_ref-plamo_30-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-plamo-30">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-:0_32-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Throughout his political career, Mulroney's fluency in English and French, with Quebec roots in both cultures, gave him an advantage that eventually proved decisive.<sup id="cite_ref-ReferenceA_2-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ReferenceA-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>By the start of 1984, as Mulroney began learning the realities of parliamentary life in the House of Commons, the Tories took a substantial lead in opinion polling. It was almost taken for granted that Trudeau would be heavily defeated by Mulroney in the general election due no later than 1985. Trudeau announced his retirement in February and <a href="/wiki/1984_Liberal_Party_of_Canada_leadership_election" title="1984 Liberal Party of Canada leadership election">was succeeded</a> as Liberal leader and prime minister by his former finance minister, <a href="/wiki/John_Turner" title="John Turner">John Turner</a>, in June. The Liberals then surged in the polls to take a lead after trailing by more than 20 percentage points. Only four days after being sworn in as prime minister, Turner called <a href="/wiki/1984_Canadian_federal_election" title="1984 Canadian federal election">a general election for September</a>. But the Liberal election campaign machinery was in disarray, leading to a weak campaign.<sup id="cite_ref-33" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-33">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the early days of the campaign, Mulroney made several gaffes regarding patronage, including the reference to Ambassador <a href="/wiki/Bryce_Mackasey" title="Bryce Mackasey">Bryce Mackasey</a> as "there's no whore like an old whore".<sup id="cite_ref-mackasey_34-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-mackasey-34">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-35" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-35">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> Most of the campaign is best remembered for his attacks on a raft of Liberal patronage appointments. In his final days in office, Trudeau had controversially appointed a flurry of <a href="/wiki/Senate_of_Canada" title="Senate of Canada">senators</a>, judges, and executives on various governmental and <a href="/wiki/Crown_corporation" class="mw-redirect" title="Crown corporation">crown corporation</a> boards, widely seen as a way to offer 'plum jobs' to loyal members of the Liberal Party. Upon assuming office, Turner had been under pressure to advise Governor General <a href="/wiki/Jeanne_Sauv%C3%A9" title="Jeanne Sauvé">Jeanne Sauvé</a> to cancel the appointments—which convention would then have required Sauvé to do. Turner did not, instead appointing several more Liberals to prominent political offices per a signed legal agreement with Trudeau.<sup id="cite_ref-36" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-36">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Ironically, Turner had planned to attack Mulroney over the patronage machine that the latter had set up in anticipation of victory. In a televised leaders' debate, Turner launched what appeared to be the start of a blistering attack on Mulroney by comparing his patronage machine to that of the old <a href="/wiki/Union_Nationale_(Quebec)" title="Union Nationale (Quebec)">Union Nationale</a> in Quebec. Mulroney successfully turned the tables by pointing to the recent raft of Liberal patronage appointments.<sup id="cite_ref-Secret7172_37-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Secret7172-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> He demanded that Turner apologize to the country for making "these horrible appointments." Turner replied that, "I had no option" except to let the appointments stand. Mulroney famously responded:
</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="/wiki/You_had_an_option,_sir" title="You had an option, sir">You had an option, sir</a>. You could have said, 'I am not going to do it. This is wrong for Canada, and I am not going to ask Canadians to pay the price.' You had an option, sir—to say 'no'—and you chose to say 'yes' to the old attitudes and the old stories of the Liberal Party. That, sir, if I may say respectfully, is not good enough for Canadians.<sup id="cite_ref-Secret7172_37-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Secret7172-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<p>Turner froze and wilted under this withering riposte from Mulroney.<sup id="cite_ref-Secret7172_37-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Secret7172-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> He could repeat only, "I had no option." A visibly angry Mulroney called this "an avowal of failure" and "a confession of non-leadership." The exchange led most papers the next day, with most of them paraphrasing Mulroney's counterattack as "You had an option, sir—you could have said 'no.'" Many observers believe that at this point, Mulroney assured himself of becoming prime minister.<sup id="cite_ref-Secret7172_37-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Secret7172-37">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On September 4, Mulroney and the Tories won the second largest majority government (in terms of percentage of seats) in Canadian history, winning 74.8 per cent of seats in the House of Commons (behind the Tories' <a href="/wiki/1958_Canadian_election" class="mw-redirect" title="1958 Canadian election">1958 landslide</a> in which they won 78.5 percent of seats). They took 211 seats, three more than their previous record in 1958 and the highest number of seats ever won by any party in Canadian history. The Liberals won only 40 seats, which, at the time, was their worst performance ever and the worst defeat for a governing party at the federal level in Canadian history. The Progressive Conservatives won just over half of the <a href="/wiki/Representative_democracy" title="Representative democracy">popular vote</a> (compared to 53.4 percent in 1958) and led in every province, emerging as a national party for the first time since 1958. Especially important was the Tories' performance in Mulroney's home province, Quebec. The Tories had only won the most seats in that province once since <a href="/wiki/1896_Canadian_federal_election" title="1896 Canadian federal election">1896</a> – the 1958 Tory landslide. Largely out of anger at Trudeau and Mulroney's promise of a new deal for Quebec, the province swung over dramatically to support him. The Tories had only won one seat out of 75 in 1980 but took 58 seats in 1984.<sup id="cite_ref-38" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-38">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup> Mulroney yielded Central Nova back to MacKay and instead ran and won in the eastern Quebec riding of <a href="/wiki/Manicouagan_(electoral_district)" title="Manicouagan (electoral district)">Manicouagan</a>, which included Baie-Comeau.<sup id="cite_ref-:0_32-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:0-32">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1984, the <a href="/wiki/Canadian_Press" class="mw-redirect" title="Canadian Press">Canadian Press</a> named Mulroney "<a href="/wiki/Newsmaker_of_the_Year" class="mw-redirect" title="Newsmaker of the Year">Newsmaker of the Year</a>" for the second straight year, making him only the second prime minister to have received the honour both before becoming prime minister and when prime minister (the other being <a href="/wiki/Lester_Pearson" class="mw-redirect" title="Lester Pearson">Lester Pearson</a>).<sup id="cite_ref-39" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-39">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Prime_Minister_.281984.E2.80.931993.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Prime_Minister_(1984–1993)">Prime Minister (1984–1993)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Brian_Mulroney&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Prime Minister (1984–1993)"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>The first Conservative majority victory in 26 years—and only the second in 54 years—initially seemed to give Mulroney a very formidable position. The Tories had won just over half the popular vote, and no other party crossed the 50-seat mark. He had wide discretion to take Canada in virtually any direction he wanted. His position was far more precarious than his parliamentary majority would suggest. Mulroney's support was based on a grand coalition of <a href="/wiki/Social_conservatism_in_Canada" title="Social conservatism in Canada">socially conservative</a> <a href="/wiki/Populists" class="mw-redirect" title="Populists">populists</a> from the West, <a href="/wiki/Quebec_nationalists" class="mw-redirect" title="Quebec nationalists">Quebec nationalists</a>, and fiscal conservatives from Ontario and Atlantic Canada. Such diverse interests became difficult for him to juggle.<sup id="cite_ref-40" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-40">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Most of Mulroney's ministers had little government experience, resulting in conflicts of interest and embarrassing scandals. Many Tories expected patronage appointments due to the long time out of government.<sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> Mulroney included a large number of Westerners in his Cabinet (including Clark as <a href="/wiki/Minister_of_Foreign_Affairs_of_Canada" class="mw-redirect" title="Minister of Foreign Affairs of Canada">minister of external affairs</a>). He was not completely successful, even aside from economic and constitutional policy. For example, he moved <a href="/wiki/CF-18" class="mw-redirect" title="CF-18">CF-18</a> servicing from Manitoba to Quebec in 1986, even though the Manitoba bid was lower and the company was better rated.<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup> Mulroney also received death threats for exerting pressure on Manitoba over French language rights.<sup id="cite_ref-Newman427_43-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Newman427-43">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Mulroney_DF-SC-85-12406.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/db/Mulroney_DF-SC-85-12406.jpg/220px-Mulroney_DF-SC-85-12406.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="146" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/db/Mulroney_DF-SC-85-12406.jpg/330px-Mulroney_DF-SC-85-12406.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/db/Mulroney_DF-SC-85-12406.jpg/440px-Mulroney_DF-SC-85-12406.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2840" data-file-height="1890" /></a><figcaption>Mila (left) and Brian (right) Mulroney at <a href="/wiki/Andrews_Air_Force_Base" title="Andrews Air Force Base">Andrews Air Force Base</a> in September 1984</figcaption></figure>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Economic_policy">Economic policy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Brian_Mulroney&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Economic policy"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Social_programs_and_spending">Social programs and spending</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Brian_Mulroney&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Social programs and spending"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>Despite Mulroney referring to social programs as a "sacred trust" when he was Opposition leader in 1983,<sup id="cite_ref-Making_medicare_–_Brian_Mulroney_44-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Making_medicare_–_Brian_Mulroney-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> he began to reduce expenditures on the programs when he came into office. In terms of <a href="/wiki/Old_age_security" class="mw-redirect" title="Old age security">old age security</a>, Mulroney's government gradually reduced its benefits at middle-income levels and above. Mulroney's government cut spending for <a href="/wiki/Unemployment_insurance" class="mw-redirect" title="Unemployment insurance">unemployment insurance</a> (UI) and reduced the range of workers covered by the benefits from the program. In their <a href="/wiki/1985_Canadian_federal_budget" title="1985 Canadian federal budget">first budget in 1985</a>, the Mulroney government announced that <a href="/wiki/Registered_home_ownership_savings_plan" title="Registered home ownership savings plan">Registered home ownership savings plan</a> (RHOSP) contributions would not be deductible if made after May 22, 1985, (funds left in the RHOSP after this date could be withdrawn tax-free, "regardless of the use"<sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;a&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-46" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-46">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTES.C._1986,_ch._6s_82_47-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTES.C._1986,_ch._6s_82-47">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup>) and no contribution could be made after December 31, 1985;<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;b&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTES.C._1986,_ch._6s_82(10)_49-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTES.C._1986,_ch._6s_82(10)-49">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> the government also announced that income earned in an RHOSP after December 31, 1985, was to be included in the owner's taxable income, effectively ending the last desirable feature of RHOSPs.<sup id="cite_ref-50" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-50">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup> In 1990, the government limited cost-sharing under the <a href="/wiki/Canada_Assistance_Plan" title="Canada Assistance Plan">Canada Assistance Plan</a> in three provinces in response to their concerns that unemployed workers would apply for cost-shared provincial social assistance (as a result of rising unemployment).<sup id="cite_ref-51" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-51">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> Also in 1990, Mulroney's government eliminated its financial contribution to UI, making all UI costs covered by worker and employer contributions.<sup id="cite_ref-CanadianLabourUI_52-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CanadianLabourUI-52">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup> In Spring 1993, the government lowered benefits for unemployed Canadians and eliminated benefits for the unemployed who failed to prove the reason they left their job. Mulroney's government limited <a href="/wiki/Baby_bonus" title="Baby bonus">baby bonus</a> payments to only lower-income Canadians.<sup id="cite_ref-LATimes_53-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-LATimes-53">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1985, Mulroney's government introduced a four-year plan to restructure family benefits. Starting in 1986, <a href="/wiki/Family_allowance" class="mw-redirect" title="Family allowance">family allowances</a> were partially <a href="/wiki/Index_(economics)" title="Index (economics)">indexed</a> to the <a href="/wiki/Cost_of_living" title="Cost of living">cost of living</a>. For three years, from 1986 to 1988, the refundable <a href="/wiki/Child_tax_credit" title="Child tax credit">child tax credits</a> were increased to $549 per year. Starting in 1989, the tax credits were partially indexed in the same manner as family allowances. That same year, as part of the government's program to target social benefits to low or middle-income Canadians, universal family allowances ended as high-income parents were required to repay all of their benefits at tax-filing time. This system maintained and increased a tax deduction for childcare expenses, benefiting high-income families the most. In 1992, the government replaced family allowances with a new Child Tax Benefit that included the family allowance, the Refundable Child Tax Credit, and a non-refundable child tax credit. The new benefit paid a maximum of $85 per month per child up to the age of 18 and was tax-free. It was income-tested on the net family income reported in the preceding year's income tax returns.<sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Mulroney's government reduced the federal workforce by 1 percent each year from 1986 to 1991, resulting in the laying off of 11,000 federal employees. Mulroney's government transferred the costs of <a href="/wiki/Universal_health_care" title="Universal health care">universal health care</a> and <a href="/wiki/Higher_education_in_Canada" title="Higher education in Canada">higher education</a> to the provinces, breaking the tradition of the two levels of government splitting the costs. As a result, some provinces had to drop insurance coverage for certain medical procedures and drugs. Mulroney's government eliminated subsidies to government-owned passenger rail and postal services, resulting in the closing of post offices in some small towns and the elimination of certain train routes. The government also introduced fees for forwarding misdirected letters. Under Mulroney, military spending growth was reduced to 1.5 per cent per year and foreign aid growth was reduced to 3 per cent per year.<sup id="cite_ref-LATimes_53-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-LATimes-53">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> Mulroney also put spending limits on <a href="/wiki/Medicare_(Canada)" title="Medicare (Canada)">medicare</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Making_medicare_–_Brian_Mulroney_44-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Making_medicare_–_Brian_Mulroney-44">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Deficit">Deficit</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Brian_Mulroney&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Deficit"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>One of Mulroney's priorities was to lower the <a href="/wiki/Government_budget_deficit" class="mw-redirect" title="Government budget deficit">deficit</a>, which under Pierre Trudeau had increased from $667 million in the <a href="/wiki/1968_Canadian_federal_budget" title="1968 Canadian federal budget">1968 budget</a> to $37.2 billion in the <a href="/wiki/1984_Canadian_federal_budget" title="1984 Canadian federal budget">1984 budget</a>. By <a href="/wiki/1988_Canadian_federal_budget" title="1988 Canadian federal budget">1988</a>, Mulroney's government cut the deficit to $28 billion, though it would never decrease beyond that point and the deficit would instead increase. The Progressive Conservatives' <a href="/wiki/1993_Canadian_federal_budget" title="1993 Canadian federal budget">final budget in 1993</a> produced a deficit of $38.5 billion, about the same level that it was when Trudeau left office. As a per cent of GDP, the deficit was reduced from 8.3 per cent to 5.6 per cent during Mulroney's tenure.<sup id="cite_ref-:CBCgraph_55-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-:CBCgraph-55">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-56" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-56">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup>
</p>

<p>The <a href="/wiki/Early_1990s_recession" title="Early 1990s recession">worldwide recession of the early 1990s</a> significantly damaged the government's financial situation. Mulroney's inability to improve the government's finances, as well as his use of tax increases to deal with it, were major factors in alienating the Western conservative portion of his power base – this contrasted with his tax cuts earlier as part of his 'pro-business' plan which had increased the deficit. At the same time, the <a href="/wiki/Bank_of_Canada" title="Bank of Canada">Bank of Canada</a> began to raise interest rates in order to meet a <a href="/wiki/Zero_inflation" class="mw-redirect" title="Zero inflation">zero inflation</a> target; the experiment was regarded as a failure that exacerbated the effect of the recession in Canada. Annual budget deficits ballooned to record levels, reaching $42 billion in his last year of office. These deficits grew the national debt dangerously close to the psychological benchmark of 100 percent of GDP, further weakening the <a href="/wiki/Canadian_dollar" title="Canadian dollar">Canadian dollar</a> and damaging Canada's international credit rating.<sup id="cite_ref-Blake_2007_57-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Blake_2007-57">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Taxation">Taxation</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Brian_Mulroney&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Taxation"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>Mulroney's government de-indexed <a href="/wiki/Income_tax_in_Canada#Personal_income_taxes" title="Income tax in Canada">personal income tax</a> <a href="/wiki/Tax_brackets" class="mw-redirect" title="Tax brackets">brackets</a> and eliminated open corporate tax loopholes. The government also increased taxes on alcohol, tobacco and gasoline.<sup id="cite_ref-LATimes_53-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-LATimes-53">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> In 1988, Mulroney's government reduced the corporate income tax from 36 percent to 28 percent.<sup id="cite_ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> That year, his government increased the <a href="/wiki/Capital_gains_tax" title="Capital gains tax">capital gains tax</a> inclusion rate from 50 percent to 66.67 percent before increasing it to 75 percent in 1990.<sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Mulroney's government passed a major tax reform bill, Bill C-139, which was made effective on January 1, 1988. It included reforms for personal and corporate income taxes. The bill expanded the tax base for personal and corporate income; lowered rates applicable to taxable income; supplanted exemptions with credits; and removed certain deductions for personal income tax. The bill replaced the 1987 rate schedule of 10 brackets (with rates ranging from 6 to 34 per cent) with a schedule of only three brackets (with rates of 17 per cent, 26 per cent, and 29 per cent). The bill also limited the lifetime capital gains exemption to $100,000; lowered capital cost allowances; established limitations on deductible business expenses; and cut the dividend tax credit.<sup id="cite_ref-60" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-60">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In August 1989, Mulroney's government announced the introduction of a nine percent national sales tax, the <a href="/wiki/Goods_and_services_tax_(Canada)" title="Goods and services tax (Canada)">goods and services tax</a> (GST), to replace the hidden 13.5 percent manufacturers' sales tax (MST). The government argued that the MST damaged the Canadian economy's competitiveness as it only applied to domestically manufactured goods, as opposed to the new GST, which applied to domestic and imported goods. The GST did not apply to basic groceries, prescription drugs, health and dental care, educational services, daycare, and legal aid. Following public backlash, Mulroney's government changed the tax rate to seven percent. Although the government argued the tax was not a tax increase, but a tax shift, the highly visible nature of the tax was extremely unpopular, and many polls showed that as many as 80 percent of Canadians were opposed to the tax. Two Progressive Conservative MPs from Alberta, <a href="/wiki/David_Kilgour" title="David Kilgour">David Kilgour</a> and <a href="/wiki/Alex_Kindy" title="Alex Kindy">Alex Kindy</a>, left the party in protest of the tax.<sup id="cite_ref-LA_Times_Canada&#39;s_9%_Sales_Tax_61-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-LA_Times_Canada&#39;s_9%_Sales_Tax-61">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-62" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-62">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Raymond_B._Blake_2007_63-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Raymond_B._Blake_2007-63">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Senate_of_Canada" title="Senate of Canada">Senate</a> with a Liberal majority refused to pass the GST. Mulroney used Section 26 (the Deadlock Clause), a little-known Constitutional provision, allowing him in an emergency situation to ask the Queen to appoint eight new senators. On September 27, 1990, at the Queen's approval, Mulroney added the eight new senators, thus giving the Tories their first majority in the Senate in nearly 50 years. In December 1990, the GST was passed in the Senate and was made effective on January 1, 1991. Mulroney's use of an "emergency" clause in the constitution was controversial and contributed to his decline in popularity.<sup id="cite_ref-Raymond_B._Blake_2007_63-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Raymond_B._Blake_2007-63">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>One of the causes of the early 1990s recession was several tax increases instituted by Mulroney's government between 1989 and 1991. The introduction of the goods and services tax and increases related to excise and payroll taxes were modelled to have reduced real GDP growth by 1.6, 2.4 and 5.1 percentage points in 1990, 1991 and 1992, respectively. Had these tax increases had not been implemented, the national debt would have increased significantly.<sup id="cite_ref-Wilson,_Dugan_and_Murphy_65-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Wilson,_Dugan_and_Murphy-65">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Privatizations">Privatizations</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Brian_Mulroney&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Privatizations"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>Mulroney's government privatized many of Canada's <a href="/wiki/Crown_corporations_of_Canada" title="Crown corporations of Canada">crown corporations</a>. In 1984, the <a href="/wiki/Government_of_Canada" title="Government of Canada">Government of Canada</a> held 61 crown corporations. Under Mulroney, it sold off 23 of them,<sup id="cite_ref-66" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-66">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup> including <a href="/wiki/Air_Canada" title="Air Canada">Air Canada</a>, which was completely privatized by 1989,<sup id="cite_ref-67" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-67">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup> although the <i>Air Canada Public Participation Act</i> continued to make certain requirements of the airline.<sup id="cite_ref-68" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-68">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup> Mulroney's government also privatized <a href="/wiki/Connaught_Laboratories" title="Connaught Laboratories">Connaught Laboratories</a> in 1984 through two public issues (one in 1984 and one in 1987)<sup id="cite_ref-69" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-69">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-70" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-70">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup> and <a href="/wiki/Petro-Canada" title="Petro-Canada">Petro-Canada</a> in 1991.<sup id="cite_ref-71" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-71">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Energy_policy">Energy policy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Brian_Mulroney&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Energy policy"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>On June 1, 1985, Mulroney's government negotiated the <a href="/wiki/Western_Canada" title="Western Canada">Western</a> Accord on Energy with the governments of the <a href="/wiki/Petroleum_industry_in_Canada" title="Petroleum industry in Canada">oil-producing</a> provinces. It permitted the full deregulation of oil prices and allowed the market forces of international and local supply and demand to determine prices. This accord abolished the <a href="/wiki/National_Energy_Program" title="National Energy Program">National Energy Program</a>, which was a policy of Trudeau's Liberal government that was highly unpopular in the Western provinces.<sup id="cite_ref-OilFreedomDay_72-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-OilFreedomDay-72">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Budget_1985_73-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Budget_1985-73">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup><sup class="reference nowrap"><span title="Page / location: 12–15">&#58;&#8202;12–15&#8202;</span></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Environmental_policy">Environmental policy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Brian_Mulroney&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Environmental policy"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The environment was a key focus of Mulroney's government. His government added eight new national parks (including <a href="/wiki/Bruce_Peninsula_National_Park" title="Bruce Peninsula National Park">Bruce Peninsula</a> and <a href="/wiki/South_Moresby" class="mw-redirect" title="South Moresby">South Moresby</a>), and passed the <i><a href="/wiki/Canadian_Environmental_Assessment_Act" class="mw-redirect" title="Canadian Environmental Assessment Act">Canadian Environmental Assessment Act</a></i> and <i><a href="/wiki/Canadian_Environmental_Protection_Act" class="mw-redirect" title="Canadian Environmental Protection Act">Canadian Environmental Protection Act</a></i>.<sup id="cite_ref-Raymond_B._Blake_2007_63-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Raymond_B._Blake_2007-63">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-74" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-74">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1987, Mulroney hosted an international climate conference in <a href="/wiki/Montreal" title="Montreal">Montreal</a>, Quebec. There, 46 nations signed the <a href="/wiki/Montreal_Protocol" title="Montreal Protocol">Montreal Protocol</a> to limit the use and production of <a href="/wiki/Chlorofluorocarbons" class="mw-redirect" title="Chlorofluorocarbons">chlorofluorocarbons</a> (CFCs); this agreement came after the discovery that CFCs were burning a hole through the <a href="/wiki/Ozone_layer" title="Ozone layer">ozone layer</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-75" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-75">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Mulroney secured the <a href="/wiki/U.S.%E2%80%93Canada_Air_Quality_Agreement" title="U.S.–Canada Air Quality Agreement">U.S.–Canada Air Quality Agreement</a>, an <a href="/wiki/Environmental_treaty" class="mw-redirect" title="Environmental treaty">environmental treaty</a> on <a href="/wiki/Acid_rain" title="Acid rain">acid rain</a>, with United States President <a href="/wiki/George_H._W._Bush" title="George H. W. Bush">George H. W. Bush</a> in 1991. Both nations committed to reducing the emissions of the air pollutants (sulphur dioxide and nitrogen oxide) that caused acid rain through a <a href="/wiki/Cap-and-trade" class="mw-redirect" title="Cap-and-trade">cap-and-trade</a> system.<sup id="cite_ref-76" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-76">&#91;74&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-77" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-77">&#91;75&#93;</a></sup> Negotiations began in 1986 when Mulroney first discussed the issue with then-president <a href="/wiki/Ronald_Reagan" title="Ronald Reagan">Ronald Reagan</a>. Mulroney repeatedly pressed the issue in public meetings with Reagan in 1987<sup id="cite_ref-78" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-78">&#91;76&#93;</a></sup> and 1988.<sup id="cite_ref-79" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-79">&#91;77&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Under Mulroney, Canada became the first industrialized country to ratify the 1992 <a href="/wiki/Convention_on_Biological_Diversity" title="Convention on Biological Diversity">Convention on Biological Diversity</a> at the <a href="/wiki/Earth_Summit" title="Earth Summit">Earth Summit</a> in Rio de Janeiro, Brazil. Conference experts claimed that Canada's signing of the treaty motivated the United Kingdom and Germany to pledge their support and thus avoid the convention's defeat. The conference also introduced the <a href="/wiki/United_Nations_Framework_Convention_on_Climate_Change" title="United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change">United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change</a>, which sought to reduce greenhouse gas emissions to an environmentally friendly level; Canada was the first <a href="/wiki/Group_of_Seven" class="mw-redirect" title="Group of Seven">Group of Seven</a> (G7) nation to sign the treaty. At the convention, Mulroney pledged $260 million from Canada toward advancing sustainable development for developing nations; this included an offer to forgive $145 million in debts owed to Canada by Latin American nations on the condition that the sum of money be used for sustainable development and social programs. At the end of the conference, Mulroney stated, "I leave this conference believing we have a better chance of saving the world than we had when we came here."<sup id="cite_ref-80" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-80">&#91;78&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-81" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-81">&#91;79&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>By 1992, Newfoundland and Labrador cod of breeding age dropped to one per cent of its estimated peak. Concerned about the <a href="/wiki/Overfishing" title="Overfishing">overfishing</a> of cod stocks off the coast of the province, Mulroney's government, in the summer of that year imposed a <a href="/wiki/Moratorium_(law)" title="Moratorium (law)">moratorium</a> on cod fishing; they initially set the ban for a minimum of two years, but later expanded it indefinitely.<sup id="cite_ref-82" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-82">&#91;80&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-83" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-83">&#91;81&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-CodEncyclopedia_84-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CodEncyclopedia-84">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup> This resulted in the layoff of 30,000–40,000 workers. Mulroney's government introduced the Northern Cod Adjustment and Recovery Program (NCARP) that provided <a href="/wiki/Unemployment_insurance" class="mw-redirect" title="Unemployment insurance">unemployment insurance</a> payments and retraining to workers; most of the workers viewed this as insufficient. In the first decade of the ban, Newfoundland and Labrador's population fell by 10 percent as people left to search for work.<sup id="cite_ref-CodEncyclopedia_84-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CodEncyclopedia-84">&#91;82&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-85" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-85">&#91;83&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Social_policy">Social policy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Brian_Mulroney&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Social policy"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>On September 22, 1988, Mulroney issued an official apology on behalf of the Canadian government for <a href="/wiki/Japanese_Canadian_internment" class="mw-redirect" title="Japanese Canadian internment">Japanese Canadian internment</a> during World War II. Mulroney's government provided a 300 million dollar compensation package, which included $21,000 to each of the remaining 13,000 survivors, $12 million for a Japanese community fund, and $24 million to create a Canadian race relations foundation.<sup id="cite_ref-86" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-86">&#91;84&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Mulroney argues he set up the <a href="/wiki/Desch%C3%AAnes_Commission" title="Deschênes Commission">Deschênes Commission</a> of inquiry on Nazi war criminals soon after he was first elected in 1984, even though it was controversial among "communities where Nazi criminals posed as respectable citizens."<sup id="cite_ref-87" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-87">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On issues of <a href="/wiki/Abortion_in_Canada" title="Abortion in Canada">abortion</a>, Mulroney declared he was opposed to "abortion on demand" but gave no details on what that meant legally.<sup id="cite_ref-88" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-88">&#91;86&#93;</a></sup> In the Spring of 1988, the Mulroney government offered a compromise solution that would give easy access to abortion in the early stages of pregnancy and criminalize late-term ones. The law in the House of Commons was defeated 147 to 76 in a <a href="/wiki/Free_vote" class="mw-redirect" title="Free vote">free vote</a>, voted against by both MPs who opposed easy access to abortions and those who opposed adding any abortion rules to the Criminal Code. Some pro-life social conservatives who played a role in Mulroney's 1984 landslide were disappointed by this move, as they were in favour of outlawing abortion completely, regardless of the circumstance. In 1989, the government introduced a much stricter bill. If enacted, it would ban all abortions unless a doctor ruled the woman's life or health would be threatened. Anyone found in violation of the law could be imprisoned for up to two years.<sup id="cite_ref-89" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-89">&#91;87&#93;</a></sup> In another free vote, the House of Commons passed the new bill by nine votes.<sup id="cite_ref-90" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-90">&#91;88&#93;</a></sup> A few months later, the bill failed in the Senate on a tie vote. Under the rules of the Senate, a tie meant the measure was defeated.<sup id="cite_ref-91" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-91">&#91;89&#93;</a></sup> This was the last time the federal government attempted to enact abortion laws. Today, abortion in Canada remains completely legal at all stages of pregnancy, regardless of the reason.<sup id="cite_ref-92" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-92">&#91;90&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1991, <i><a href="/wiki/Frank_(magazine)" title="Frank (magazine)">Frank</a></i> magazine ran a satirical advertisement for a contest inviting young Tories to "Deflower <a href="/wiki/Caroline_Mulroney" title="Caroline Mulroney">Caroline Mulroney</a>." Her father was incensed and threatened physical harm toward those responsible before joining several women's groups in denouncing the ad as an incitement to rape on national television. Frank's editor <a href="/wiki/Michael_Bate" title="Michael Bate">Michael Bate</a>, called the spoof, intended to mock her unpopular father for bringing her to public adult-oriented events, "clumsy" but had no regrets. Bate also shared sympathy toward her father's reaction over the spoof.<sup id="cite_ref-93" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-93">&#91;91&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Attempted_constitutional_reform">Attempted constitutional reform</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Brian_Mulroney&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: Attempted constitutional reform"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Meech_Lake_Accord">Meech Lake Accord</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Brian_Mulroney&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: Meech Lake Accord"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033289096">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Meech_Lake_Accord" title="Meech Lake Accord">Meech Lake Accord</a></div>
<p>A major undertaking by Mulroney's government was an attempt to resolve the divisive issue of national unity. In 1981, Quebec Premier <a href="/wiki/Ren%C3%A9_L%C3%A9vesque" title="René Lévesque">René Lévesque</a>, leader of the Quebec nationalist <a href="/wiki/Parti_Qu%C3%A9b%C3%A9cois" title="Parti Québécois">Parti Québécois</a> government, had been the only provincial premier not to agree to the package of constitutional amendments which <a href="/wiki/Patriated" class="mw-redirect" title="Patriated">patriated</a> the <a href="/wiki/Constitution_of_Canada" title="Constitution of Canada">Constitution of Canada</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-94" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-94">&#91;92&#93;</a></sup> In the <a href="/wiki/1985_Quebec_general_election" title="1985 Quebec general election">1985 Quebec provincial election</a>, the Parti Québécois government suffered a landslide defeat to the <a href="/wiki/Quebec_Liberal_Party" title="Quebec Liberal Party">Liberals</a> led by <a href="/wiki/Robert_Bourassa" title="Robert Bourassa">Robert Bourassa</a>.
