Some believed that the new Quebec government's moderate stance on nationalism would allow the province to formally endorse the constitution. Mulroney wanted Quebec to endorse the constitution and wanted to include Quebec in a new agreement with the rest of Canada.<sup id="cite_ref-MeechLakeEncyclopedia_95-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MeechLakeEncyclopedia-95">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup> In August 1986, Mulroney met with <a href="/wiki/Premier_(Canada)" title="Premier (Canada)">provincial premiers</a> in Edmonton, Alberta, where the ministers agreed to the "Edmonton Declaration". It stated that a "Quebec Round" of constitutional talks based on Bourassa's five conditions that would have to be met for Quebec's endorsement of the constitution (recognition of Quebec's distinct character (as primarily Catholic and French-speaking); a veto for Quebec in constitutional matters; input from Quebec into the appointment of Supreme Court justices; entrenchment of Quebec's role in immigration; and a limit on the federal spending power<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMacDonald249_96-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMacDonald249-96">&#91;94&#93;</a></sup>) would occur before further reforms would be undertaken.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMacDonald251–252_97-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMacDonald251–252-97">&#91;95&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Mulroney called a <a href="/wiki/First_Ministers%27_conference" title="First Ministers&#39; conference">First Ministers' conference</a> with the ten provincial premiers for April 30, 1987, at Willson House, located on the shores of <a href="/wiki/Meech_Lake" title="Meech Lake">Meech Lake</a>, Quebec, in the <a href="/wiki/Gatineau_Hills" title="Gatineau Hills">Gatineau Hills</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMacDonald253_98-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMacDonald253-98">&#91;96&#93;</a></sup> During the conference, Mulroney negotiated the <a href="/wiki/Meech_Lake_Accord" title="Meech Lake Accord">Meech Lake Accord</a>, a package of constitutional amendments designed to satisfy Quebec's demand for recognition as a "<a href="/wiki/Distinct_society" title="Distinct society">distinct society</a>" within Canada. The Accord also devolved some powers to the provinces such as giving provinces a role in nominating people to serve in some federal institutions (e.g. the <a href="/wiki/Senate_of_Canada" title="Senate of Canada">Senate</a> and <a href="/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_Canada" title="Supreme Court of Canada">Supreme Court of Canada</a>); allowing provinces to withdraw from federally-financed social programs on the conditions that the province establish its own program that meets national standards; giving constitutional status to federal–provincial immigration agreements; and mandating annual First Ministers' conferences (the Accord also required that <a href="/wiki/Senate_of_Canada#Senate_reform" title="Senate of Canada">Senate reform</a> and <a href="/wiki/Fishing_industry_in_Canada" title="Fishing industry in Canada">fisheries</a> be discussed at the conferences), which made the federal-provincial consultative process constitutional.<sup id="cite_ref-MeechLakeEncyclopedia_95-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MeechLakeEncyclopedia-95">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup> At a final roll call at 4:45&#160;a.m. on June 3, 1987, hours before the signing ceremony, Mulroney knowingly breached convention by taking the vote in reverse order around the table instead of the traditional order of a province's entry into confederation.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMacDonald280_99-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMacDonald280-99">&#91;97&#93;</a></sup> At the symbolic signing ceremony, the premiers signed the Accord.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMacDonald281_100-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMacDonald281-100">&#91;98&#93;</a></sup> The agreement would have changed the <a href="/wiki/Amendments_to_the_Constitution_of_Canada" title="Amendments to the Constitution of Canada">constitution's amending formula</a>. It therefore needed to be ratified by the <a href="/wiki/Parliament_of_Canada" title="Parliament of Canada">federal parliament</a> and the legislatures of all ten provinces.<sup id="cite_ref-101" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-101">&#91;99&#93;</a></sup> As well, other parts of the Accord were made under the general amendment provision.<sup id="cite_ref-102" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-102">&#91;100&#93;</a></sup>  That meant that there was a three-year deadline for those amendments to pass.<sup id="cite_ref-103" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-103">&#91;101&#93;</a></sup>  On June 23, 1987, Quebec became the first province to approve of the Accord, triggering the three-year time limit provided for by the Section 39(2) of the <i>Constitution Act, 1982</i>; this meant that June 22, 1990, would be the last possible day the Accord could pass.<sup id="cite_ref-104" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-104">&#91;102&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Opinion polls showed that a majority of Canadians supported the Accord. Some believed the Accord would weaken <a href="/wiki/Quebec_separatism" class="mw-redirect" title="Quebec separatism">Quebec separatism</a>. Critics believed the Accord would weaken the federal government's authority, and some from <a href="/wiki/English_Canada" title="English Canada">English Canada</a> argued the "distinct society" clause would give Quebec special status and not make it equal to the other nine provinces. Mulroney told the <i><a href="/wiki/Toronto_Star" title="Toronto Star">Toronto Star</a></i>, "You can have the old style of warring federalism, or you can have genuine co-operative federalism, on which we're trying to build a new country."<sup id="cite_ref-105" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-105">&#91;103&#93;</a></sup> As criticism grew, support for the Accord declined outside of Quebec; some there feared its failure would spark a backlash in the province and damage national unity.<sup id="cite_ref-MeechLakeEncyclopedia_95-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MeechLakeEncyclopedia-95">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>A commission headed by Mulroney's former cabinet minister, <a href="/wiki/Jean_Charest" title="Jean Charest">Jean Charest</a>, recommended a companion accord that would address the concerns of other provinces, assert that the distinct society clause would be subject to the <i>Charter</i>, and would feature greater protections for minority language rights in the provinces.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMacDonald303_106-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMacDonald303-106">&#91;104&#93;</a></sup> Mulroney's <a href="/wiki/Minister_of_Environment_and_Climate_Change" title="Minister of Environment and Climate Change">environment minister</a> and <a href="/wiki/Quebec_lieutenant" title="Quebec lieutenant">Quebec lieutenant</a>, <a href="/wiki/Lucien_Bouchard" title="Lucien Bouchard">Lucien Bouchard</a>, viewed the companion accord as a betrayal of Meech and subsequently praised the Parti Québécois in a telegram. Mulroney reportedly demanded Bouchard clarify the remark or resign, and Bouchard supplied a lengthy letter of resignation on May 22, 1990.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMacDonald304–305_107-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMacDonald304–305-107">&#91;105&#93;</a></sup> Mulroney claimed he fired Bouchard. Bouchard left the Progressive Conservatives soon afterward. After the failure of the Accord, Bouchard convinced several other Tories and Liberals to join him to form the <a href="/wiki/Bloc_Qu%C3%A9b%C3%A9cois" title="Bloc Québécois">Bloc Québécois</a>, a pro-sovereigntist party.<sup id="cite_ref-CBCAug2014_108-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CBCAug2014-108">&#91;106&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-G&amp;MNov2015_109-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-G&amp;MNov2015-109">&#91;107&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-CTV_110-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CTV-110">&#91;108&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In early June 1990, all premiers finally agreed to ratify the Accord<sup id="cite_ref-111" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-111">&#91;note 1&#93;</a></sup> provided there be further constitutional discussions revolving around an elected Senate, the amending formula, equality, and Indigenous issues. Around that time, New Brunswick agreed to ratify the agreement. Manitoba and, Newfoundland, and Labrador remained the only provinces to have not ratified it; they only had a few weeks left. Unanimous support from every member of the <a href="/wiki/Manitoba_Legislative_Assembly" class="mw-redirect" title="Manitoba Legislative Assembly">Manitoba Legislative Assembly</a> was required to bypass the necessary public consultations in the assembly and proceed with ratification. On June 12, 1990, Member of the Legislative Assembly of Manitoba <a href="/wiki/Elijah_Harper" title="Elijah Harper">Elijah Harper</a> announced his opposition to the Accord on the grounds that <a href="/wiki/Indigenous_peoples_in_Canada" title="Indigenous peoples in Canada">Indigenous groups</a> had not been consulted. Harper's opposition prevented the amendment from proceeding; thus, the Accord failed to pass in the legislature.<sup id="cite_ref-MeechLakeEncyclopedia_95-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MeechLakeEncyclopedia-95">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-112" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-112">&#91;109&#93;</a></sup> This allowed the <a href="/wiki/Premier_of_Newfoundland_and_Labrador" title="Premier of Newfoundland and Labrador">premier of Newfoundland and Labrador</a>, <a href="/wiki/Clyde_Kirby_Wells" class="mw-redirect" title="Clyde Kirby Wells">Clyde Wells</a> (who revoked the province's previous assent though reluctantly agreed to ratify the Accord in June 1990<sup id="cite_ref-113" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-113">&#91;110&#93;</a></sup>), to excuse himself from bringing the Accord to a vote in the <a href="/wiki/Newfoundland_and_Labrador_House_of_Assembly" title="Newfoundland and Labrador House of Assembly">Newfoundland and Labrador House of Assembly</a>. The Accord failed to be ratified as Manitoba and Newfoundland and Labrador did not approve of it by the June 23, 1990, deadline.<sup id="cite_ref-MeechLakeEncyclopedia_95-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MeechLakeEncyclopedia-95">&#91;93&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Charlottetown_Accord">Charlottetown Accord</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Brian_Mulroney&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: Charlottetown Accord"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Charlottetown_Accord" title="Charlottetown Accord">Charlottetown Accord</a></div>
<p>Following the Meech Lake failure, Mulroney sought a second attempt to get Quebec's endorsement of the constitution. He appointed his foreign minister, <a href="/wiki/Joe_Clark" title="Joe Clark">Joe Clark</a>, as the first <a href="/wiki/Minister_responsible_for_Constitutional_Affairs" title="Minister responsible for Constitutional Affairs">minister responsible for constitutional affairs</a> on April 21, 1991.<sup id="cite_ref-114" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-114">&#91;111&#93;</a></sup> Clark was responsible for establishing a new accord to end the constitutional deadlock with Quebec. Mulroney's government appointed two Quebec bodies (the <a href="/wiki/Allaire_Report" title="Allaire Report">Allaire Committee</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Commission_on_the_Political_and_Constitutional_Future_of_Quebec" title="Commission on the Political and Constitutional Future of Quebec">Belanger-Campeau Committee</a>) and two national bodies (the Beaudoin-Edwards Committee and the <a href="/wiki/Spicer_Commission" class="mw-redirect" title="Spicer Commission">Spicer Commission</a>) to engage in discussions regarding constitutional reform. These bodies generated various reports, including the federal document titled <i>Shaping Canada's Future Together</i>. The Mulroney government then held five national conferences to discuss the proposals in the document. The conferences led to another federal report titled <i>A Renewed Canada.</i>. Afterward, negotiations between the federal, provincial, and territorial governments occurred. Unlike the Meech Lake Accord, Indigenous peoples were included in the discussions. Quebec was included in the final stages. The negotiations culminated in the <a href="/wiki/Charlottetown_Accord" title="Charlottetown Accord">Charlottetown Accord</a>, which was unveiled in <a href="/wiki/Charlottetown" title="Charlottetown">Charlottetown</a>, Prince Edward Island, on August 28, 1992.<sup id="cite_ref-CharlottetownEncyclopedia_115-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CharlottetownEncyclopedia-115">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Accord gave provinces jurisdiction over <a href="/wiki/Forestry_in_Canada" title="Forestry in Canada">foresty</a>, <a href="/wiki/Mining_in_Canada" title="Mining in Canada">mining</a>, <a href="/wiki/Culture_of_Canada" title="Culture of Canada">cultural affairs</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-116" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-116">&#91;note 2&#93;</a></sup> and other areas; required the federal government to negotiate policy with the provinces in certain areas such as telecommunications, labour and training, regional development, and immigration; abolished <a href="/wiki/Disallowance_and_reservation_in_Canada" title="Disallowance and reservation in Canada">disallowance</a> (which gives the federal <a href="/wiki/Cabinet_of_Canada" title="Cabinet of Canada">Cabinet</a> power to overrule provincial legislation within one year of it being passed); and required provincial consent for the federal government gaining power over provincial infrastructure projects. The Accord allowed provinces to create their own social programs and mandated the federal government to compensate provinces as long as the provincial social programs met national standards. It also mandated the federal government to compensate provinces that withdrew from any constitutional amendment that transferred provincial powers to the federal government; the compensation would allow provinces to fund their own programs. In addition, the Accord addressed <a href="/wiki/Indigenous_self-government_in_Canada" title="Indigenous self-government in Canada">Indigenous self-government</a> and contained the "Canada Clause" that determines Canadian values including egalitarianism, <a href="/wiki/Multiculturalism_in_Canada" title="Multiculturalism in Canada">multiculturalism</a>, and recognition of Quebec as a distinct society. Finally, the Accord entrenched the structure and appointment process for the <a href="/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_Canada" title="Supreme Court of Canada">Supreme Court of Canada</a> in the constitution; changed the Senate into a <a href="/wiki/Triple-E_Senate" title="Triple-E Senate">Triple-E Senate</a> with reduced powers (such as requiring a majority of all senators and a majority of <a href="/wiki/Francophone_Canadians" title="Francophone Canadians">Francophone</a> senators in certain votes); increased the number of seats in the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Commons" title="House of Commons">House of Commons</a>; guaranteed Quebec at least a quarter of the Commons' seats; and increased the number of matters that require unanimous approval for a constitutional amendment.<sup id="cite_ref-CharlottetownEncyclopedia_115-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CharlottetownEncyclopedia-115">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Accord was supported by the federal government and all ten provincial governments. Although it could have been ratified as a constitutional amendment, Mulroney's government insisted on holding a national <a href="/wiki/Referendum" title="Referendum">referendum</a> to avoid a repetition of the criticism that the Meech Lake Accord was agreed upon without public approval. On October 26, 1992, two referendums, one national (without Quebec) and one in Quebec, were held, asking if Canadians agreed with the Charlottetown Accord. Nationally, 54.3 percent opposed the Accord. In Quebec, 56.7 percent opposed it. Many saw the Accord's defeat as a protest against Mulroney's government, which was heavily unpopular due to the failure of the previous Meech Lake Accord, the introduction of the GST, and the early 1990s recession.<sup id="cite_ref-CharlottetownEncyclopedia_115-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CharlottetownEncyclopedia-115">&#91;112&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Foreign_policy">Foreign policy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Brian_Mulroney&amp;action=edit&amp;section=20" title="Edit section: Foreign policy"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>As prime minister, Mulroney strengthened Canada's relations with the United States, moving away from Pierre Trudeau's <a href="/wiki/Third_Option" title="Third Option">Third Option</a> policy of reducing American influence on Canada. Mulroney established a close relationship with U.S. President Ronald Reagan.<sup id="cite_ref-117" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-117">&#91;113&#93;</a></sup> On March 17 and 18, 1985, the "<a href="/wiki/Shamrock_Summit" title="Shamrock Summit">Shamrock Summit</a>" between Mulroney and Reagan was held in <a href="/wiki/Quebec_City" title="Quebec City">Quebec City</a>. The summit gained its name from the two leaders' Irish background, and because the meeting started on <a href="/wiki/St._Patrick%27s_Day" class="mw-redirect" title="St. Patrick&#39;s Day">St. Patrick's Day</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-118" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-118">&#91;114&#93;</a></sup> In the summit, Mulroney and Reagan sang <i><a href="/wiki/When_Irish_Eyes_are_Smiling" class="mw-redirect" title="When Irish Eyes are Smiling">When Irish Eyes are Smiling</a></i>; this signified the camaraderie between the two leaders and a turning point in <a href="/wiki/Canada%E2%80%93United_States_relations" title="Canada–United States relations">Canada–United States relations</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-119" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-119">&#91;115&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-120" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-120">&#91;116&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-121" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-121">&#91;117&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The <a href="/wiki/Air_India_Flight_182" title="Air India Flight 182">Air India Flight 182</a> bombing, which originated in <a href="/wiki/Montreal" title="Montreal">Montreal</a>, occurred on June 23, 1985. This was the largest terrorist act before <a href="/wiki/September_11,_2001" class="mw-redirect" title="September 11, 2001">September 11, 2001</a>, with the majority of the 329 victims being Canadian citizens. Mulroney sent a letter of condolence to Indian Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Rajiv_Gandhi" title="Rajiv Gandhi">Rajiv Gandhi</a>, sparking an uproar in Canada since he did not call the families of the actual victims to offer condolences. Furthermore, there were several warnings from the <a href="/wiki/Indian_government" class="mw-redirect" title="Indian government">Indian government</a> to the Mulroney government about terrorist threats toward Air India flights. Questions remain as to why these warnings were not taken more seriously and whether the events leading to the bombing could have been prevented.<sup id="cite_ref-story_122-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-story-122">&#91;118&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-ctv_story_123-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ctv_story-123">&#91;119&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-124" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-124">&#91;120&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In November 1984, Mulroney sent his newly appointed <a href="/wiki/Permanent_Representative_of_Canada_to_the_United_Nations" title="Permanent Representative of Canada to the United Nations">Canadian ambassador to the United Nations</a>, <a href="/wiki/Stephen_Lewis" title="Stephen Lewis">Stephen Lewis</a>, to the <a href="/wiki/Headquarters_of_the_United_Nations" title="Headquarters of the United Nations">Headquarters of the United Nations</a> in New York City to persuade the <a href="/wiki/United_Nations_General_Assembly" title="United Nations General Assembly">General Assembly</a> to take action against <a href="/wiki/1983%E2%80%931985_famine_in_Ethiopia" title="1983–1985 famine in Ethiopia">the ongoing Ethiopian famine</a>. Days later, the UN and <a href="/wiki/Red_Cross" class="mw-redirect" title="Red Cross">Red Cross</a> launched an effort to save seven million starving Ethiopians and 22 million others in Africa. The Mulroney government's efforts to aid Ethiopia differed Canada from the United States and the United Kingdom, two Western nations which avoided taking action against the famine due to Ethiopia's <a href="/wiki/Marxist" class="mw-redirect" title="Marxist">Marxist</a> regime. Mulroney's foreign affairs minister, <a href="/wiki/Joe_Clark" title="Joe Clark">Joe Clark</a>, became the first senior Western official to visit Ethiopia during the famine, ahead of UN officials. Mulroney's government spent tens of millions of dollars to match private donations to combat the famine. Canada contributed to over 10 percent of international aid to Ethiopia. After the famine, Mulroney's government increased aid and development funding to Africa.<sup id="cite_ref-125" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-125">&#91;121&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Photograph_of_The_Reagans_and_Mulroneys_in_Quebec,_Canada_-_NARA_-_198561.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dc/Photograph_of_The_Reagans_and_Mulroneys_in_Quebec%2C_Canada_-_NARA_-_198561.jpg/220px-Photograph_of_The_Reagans_and_Mulroneys_in_Quebec%2C_Canada_-_NARA_-_198561.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="280" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dc/Photograph_of_The_Reagans_and_Mulroneys_in_Quebec%2C_Canada_-_NARA_-_198561.jpg/330px-Photograph_of_The_Reagans_and_Mulroneys_in_Quebec%2C_Canada_-_NARA_-_198561.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dc/Photograph_of_The_Reagans_and_Mulroneys_in_Quebec%2C_Canada_-_NARA_-_198561.jpg/440px-Photograph_of_The_Reagans_and_Mulroneys_in_Quebec%2C_Canada_-_NARA_-_198561.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2361" data-file-height="3000" /></a><figcaption>The Mulroneys with the Reagans in <a href="/wiki/Quebec_City" title="Quebec City">Quebec City</a>, Canada, on March 18, 1985, the second day of the "<a href="/wiki/Shamrock_Summit" title="Shamrock Summit">Shamrock Summit</a>."</figcaption></figure>
<p>On December 2, 1991, Canada became the first Western nation to recognize Ukraine as an independent country, next day after the <a href="/wiki/1991_Ukrainian_independence_referendum" title="1991 Ukrainian independence referendum">landslide referendum in favour of independence in Ukraine</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-126" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-126">&#91;122&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Apartheid">Apartheid</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Brian_Mulroney&amp;action=edit&amp;section=21" title="Edit section: Apartheid"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>Mulroney's government opposed the <a href="/wiki/Apartheid" title="Apartheid">apartheid</a> regime in South Africa. Beginning in 1985, Mulroney led an effort within the <a href="/wiki/Commonwealth_of_Nations" title="Commonwealth of Nations">Commonwealth</a> to <a href="/wiki/Economic_sanctions" title="Economic sanctions">sanction</a> the South African government with the goal of pressuring them to end apartheid and release <a href="/wiki/Internal_resistance_to_apartheid" title="Internal resistance to apartheid">anti-apartheid</a> activist <a href="/wiki/Nelson_Mandela" title="Nelson Mandela">Nelson Mandela</a> from prison. This put Mulroney at odds with British Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Margaret_Thatcher" title="Margaret Thatcher">Margaret Thatcher</a>, who opposed apartheid but believed the sanctions would hurt Britain (which had economic ties with South Africa) and that the measures would hurt South Africans and cause job losses. United States President Ronald Reagan also opposed sanctions, believing that Mandela and other leaders of the <a href="/wiki/African_National_Congress" title="African National Congress">African National Congress</a> were communists.<sup id="cite_ref-NaPoapartheid_127-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-NaPoapartheid-127">&#91;123&#93;</a></sup> At an October 23, 1985, <a href="/wiki/United_Nations_General_Assembly" title="United Nations General Assembly">United Nations General Assembly</a> meeting, Mulroney stated, "if there is no progress in the dismantling of apartheid, <a href="/wiki/Canada%E2%80%93South_Africa_relations" title="Canada–South Africa relations">[Canada]'s relations with South Africa</a> may have to be severed completely"; he restored this line in his speech after he originally removed it at the advice of External Affairs.<sup id="cite_ref-128" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-128">&#91;124&#93;</a></sup> After an August 1986 meeting in London, Canada, along with other Commonwealth nations, implemented 11 new sanctions on South Africa including bans on new air links, new investment, promotion of <a href="/wiki/Tourism_in_South_Africa" title="Tourism in South Africa">tourism</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-NaPoapartheid_127-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-NaPoapartheid-127">&#91;123&#93;</a></sup> and imports of South African coal, metals and agricultural goods.<sup id="cite_ref-129" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-129">&#91;125&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The day after Mandela was released on February 11, 1990, he spoke with Mulroney through the telephone, thanking him for his efforts to end apartheid. Mandela made the <a href="/wiki/Parliament_of_Canada" title="Parliament of Canada">Parliament of Canada</a> his first legislature in the world to make a speech. On June 18 that year, Mandela spoke in the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Commons_of_Canada" title="House of Commons of Canada">House of Commons of Canada</a>, where he thanked Mulroney and Canadians. The two remained in contact after they left politics; during his annual business trips to South Africa, Mulroney visited Mandela.<sup id="cite_ref-NaPoapartheid_127-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-NaPoapartheid-127">&#91;123&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Free_trade_and_1988_re-election">Free trade and 1988 re-election</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Brian_Mulroney&amp;action=edit&amp;section=22" title="Edit section: Free trade and 1988 re-election"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>Critics noted that Mulroney had originally professed opposition to <a href="/wiki/Free_trade" title="Free trade">free trade</a> during the 1983 leadership campaign<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated1_130-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated1-130">&#91;126&#93;</a></sup> though the 1985 report of the <a href="/wiki/MacDonald_Commission" class="mw-redirect" title="MacDonald Commission">MacDonald Commission</a> suggested free trade as an idea to him.<sup id="cite_ref-131" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-131">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup> Negotiations between Canada and the United States for a <a href="/wiki/Free_trade_treaty" class="mw-redirect" title="Free trade treaty">free trade treaty</a> started in May 1986. In October 1987, a deal was reached;<sup id="cite_ref-132" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-132">&#91;128&#93;</a></sup> the <a href="/wiki/Canada%E2%80%93United_States_Free_Trade_Agreement" title="Canada–United States Free Trade Agreement">Canada–United States Free Trade Agreement</a> (CUSFTA) stated that all <a href="/wiki/Tariff" title="Tariff">tariffs</a> between the two countries would be eliminated by 1998. This deal was achieved not least because of Mulroney's close relationship with U.S. President Ronald Reagan.<sup id="cite_ref-133" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-133">&#91;129&#93;</a></sup> This agreement was controversial; while Mulroney used his massive majority in the House of Commons to pass the bill, the <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_of_Canada" title="Liberal Party of Canada">Liberal</a>-dominated <a href="/wiki/Senate_of_Canada" title="Senate of Canada">Senate</a> demanded an election before proceeding to a ratification vote.<sup id="cite_ref-134" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-134">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup> This induced Mulroney to ask <a href="/wiki/Governor_General_of_Canada" title="Governor General of Canada">Governor General</a> <a href="/wiki/Jeanne_Sauv%C3%A9" title="Jeanne Sauvé">Jeanne Sauvé</a> on October 1 to dissolve <a href="/wiki/33rd_Canadian_Parliament" title="33rd Canadian Parliament">Parliament</a> and call <a href="/wiki/1988_Canadian_federal_election" title="1988 Canadian federal election">an election for November 21</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-1988CanadianElection_135-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-1988CanadianElection-135">&#91;131&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:President_Bush,_Canadian_Prime_Minister_Brian_Mulroney_and_Mexican_President_Carlos_Salinas_participate_in_the..._-_NARA_-_186460.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2d/President_Bush%2C_Canadian_Prime_Minister_Brian_Mulroney_and_Mexican_President_Carlos_Salinas_participate_in_the..._-_NARA_-_186460.jpg/220px-President_Bush%2C_Canadian_Prime_Minister_Brian_Mulroney_and_Mexican_President_Carlos_Salinas_participate_in_the..._-_NARA_-_186460.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="137" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2d/President_Bush%2C_Canadian_Prime_Minister_Brian_Mulroney_and_Mexican_President_Carlos_Salinas_participate_in_the..._-_NARA_-_186460.jpg/330px-President_Bush%2C_Canadian_Prime_Minister_Brian_Mulroney_and_Mexican_President_Carlos_Salinas_participate_in_the..._-_NARA_-_186460.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2d/President_Bush%2C_Canadian_Prime_Minister_Brian_Mulroney_and_Mexican_President_Carlos_Salinas_participate_in_the..._-_NARA_-_186460.jpg/440px-President_Bush%2C_Canadian_Prime_Minister_Brian_Mulroney_and_Mexican_President_Carlos_Salinas_participate_in_the..._-_NARA_-_186460.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2601" data-file-height="1617" /></a><figcaption>NAFTA Initialling Ceremony, October 1992; From left to right: (Standing) Mexican President <a href="/wiki/Carlos_Salinas" class="mw-redirect" title="Carlos Salinas">Salinas</a>, US President <a href="/wiki/George_H._W._Bush" title="George H. W. Bush">Bush</a>, Prime Minister Mulroney, (Seated) <a href="/wiki/Jaime_Serra_Puche" title="Jaime Serra Puche">Jaime Serra Puche</a>, <a href="/wiki/Carla_Hills" class="mw-redirect" title="Carla Hills">Carla Hills</a>, <a href="/wiki/Michael_Wilson_(Canadian_politician)" title="Michael Wilson (Canadian politician)">Michael Wilson</a>.</figcaption></figure>
<p>The trade deal was the main issue of the election, with the Liberals and <a href="/wiki/New_Democratic_Party" title="New Democratic Party">New Democratic Party</a> (NDP) opposing it; Liberal leader <a href="/wiki/John_Turner" title="John Turner">John Turner</a> (who was preparing for his second campaign after Mulroney defeated him in <a href="/wiki/1984_Canadian_federal_election" title="1984 Canadian federal election">1984</a>) believed that the agreement would "Americanize" Canada and cost many Canadian jobs. A week after the October 25 debate, the Liberals were six points ahead of the PCs. To combat this Liberal surge, the PCs began running a more negative campaign, capitalizing on the perceived lack of public confidence in Turner, his perceived inability to lead the Liberal Party, and arguing that he only opposed free trade because of political opportunism. The Progressive Conservatives poll numbers began to rebound, and they were re-elected with a greatly reduced majority, winning 169 out of 295 seats and 43 percent of the popular vote.<sup id="cite_ref-1988CanadianElection_135-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-1988CanadianElection-135">&#91;131&#93;</a></sup> Mulroney became the first and only federal Canadian Conservative party leader since <a href="/wiki/John_A._Macdonald" title="John A. Macdonald">John A. Macdonald</a> to lead his party to a second majority government.<sup id="cite_ref-136" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-136">&#91;c&#93;</a></sup> The trade deal gained the support of Quebec Premier <a href="/wiki/Robert_Bourassa" title="Robert Bourassa">Robert Bourassa</a>, which helped the PCs maintain their standing in Quebec.<sup id="cite_ref-137" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-137">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup> In this election, Mulroney transferred to another eastern Quebec seat, <a href="/wiki/Charlevoix_(federal_electoral_district)" title="Charlevoix (federal electoral district)">Charlevoix</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-138" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-138">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Also on November 21, Mulroney made a controversial <a href="/wiki/Order_in_Council" title="Order in Council">Order in Council</a> which allowed the establishment of the AMEX Bank of Canada (owned by <a href="/wiki/American_Express" title="American Express">American Express</a>), despite Finance Minister <a href="/wiki/Michael_Wilson_(Canadian_politician)" title="Michael Wilson (Canadian politician)">Michael Wilson</a> rejecting AMEX's application to open a Canadian bank in 1986. The Amex Bank of Canada started operating on July 1, 1990. <a href="/wiki/Toronto-Dominion_Bank" title="Toronto-Dominion Bank">Toronto-Dominion Bank</a> chairman Richard Thomson accused Mulroney's government of favouritism toward Amex as its Chief Executive Officer, James Robinson, supported free trade.<sup id="cite_ref-139" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-139">&#91;134&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The government implemented the deal; it was made effective on January 1, 1989. In 1994, CUSFTA was replaced by the <a href="/wiki/North_American_Free_Trade_Agreement" title="North American Free Trade Agreement">North American Free Trade Agreement</a> (NAFTA), which now included Mexico.<sup id="cite_ref-140" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-140">&#91;135&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Gulf_War">Gulf War</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Brian_Mulroney&amp;action=edit&amp;section=23" title="Edit section: Gulf War"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>In the early 1990s, Mulroney played a vital part in upholding international law to stop <a href="/wiki/Saddam_Hussein" title="Saddam Hussein">Saddam Hussein</a>'s aggression in <a href="/wiki/Kuwait" title="Kuwait">Kuwait</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-dewing00_141-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-dewing00-141">&#91;136&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-hampson_142-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-hampson-142">&#91;137&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHampson2018_143-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHampson2018-143">&#91;138&#93;</a></sup> Along with Ambassador <a href="/wiki/Yves_Fortier_(lawyer)" class="mw-redirect" title="Yves Fortier (lawyer)">Yves Fortier</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-hampson_142-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-hampson-142">&#91;137&#93;</a></sup> Mulroney was instrumental in drafting <a href="/wiki/UNSCR_678" class="mw-redirect" title="UNSCR 678">UNSCR 678</a> which later led to the war when Iraq failed to heed the resolution,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENewman200510_144-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENewman200510-144">&#91;139&#93;</a></sup> and Canada supported the <a href="/wiki/Desert_Storm" class="mw-redirect" title="Desert Storm">armed UN coalition</a> during the 1991 <a href="/wiki/Gulf_War" title="Gulf War">Gulf War</a> through Operation SCIMITAR and through Operation FRICTION.<sup id="cite_ref-oscim_145-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-oscim-145">&#91;140&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-146" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-146">&#91;141&#93;</a></sup> When the UN authorized full use of force in the operation, Canada sent a <a href="/wiki/CF-18" class="mw-redirect" title="CF-18">CF-18</a> squadron with support personnel and a field hospital to deal with casualties from the ground war as well as a company of <a href="/wiki/The_Royal_Canadian_Regiment" class="mw-redirect" title="The Royal Canadian Regiment">The Royal Canadian Regiment</a> to safeguard these ground elements. The <a href="/wiki/Canadian_Forces" class="mw-redirect" title="Canadian Forces">Canadian Forces</a> code-named Canada's participation <a href="/wiki/Operation_Friction" title="Operation Friction">Operation Friction</a>. In August, Mulroney sent the destroyers <a href="/wiki/HMCS_Terra_Nova" title="HMCS Terra Nova">HMCS <i>Terra Nova</i></a> and <a href="/wiki/HMCS_Athabaskan_(DDG_282)" title="HMCS Athabaskan (DDG 282)">HMCS <i>Athabaskan</i></a> to enforce the trade blockade against Iraq. The supply ship <a href="/wiki/HMCS_Protecteur" title="HMCS Protecteur">HMCS <i>Protecteur</i></a> was also sent to aid the gathering coalition forces. When the air war began, Canada's planes were integrated into the coalition force and, provided air cover and attacked ground targets. This was the first time since the fighting on Cyprus in 1974 that Canadian forces participated directly in combat operations.<sup id="cite_ref-147" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-147">&#91;142&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Unpopularity_and_retirement">Unpopularity and retirement</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Brian_Mulroney&amp;action=edit&amp;section=24" title="Edit section: Unpopularity and retirement"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Fracturing_of_electoral_coalition">Fracturing of electoral coalition</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Brian_Mulroney&amp;action=edit&amp;section=25" title="Edit section: Fracturing of electoral coalition"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>In late 1987, the Western Canada-based <a href="/wiki/Right-wing_populist" class="mw-redirect" title="Right-wing populist">right-wing populist</a> <a href="/wiki/Reform_Party_of_Canada" title="Reform Party of Canada">Reform Party of Canada</a> was founded. The creation of the party was motivated by Western Canadian discontent with Mulroney's government and the Progressive Conservatives in general. The Reform Party opposed the Mulroney government's promotion of the Meech Lake and Charlottetown Accords as well as their introduction of the goods and services tax. Although the party won only 2 percent of the popular vote and no seats in the 1988 election, it won its first seat in the Commons on <a href="/wiki/1989_Beaver_River_federal_by-election" title="1989 Beaver River federal by-election">the May 6, 1989, by-election</a> in the Alberta riding of <a href="/wiki/Beaver_River_(federal_electoral_district)" title="Beaver River (federal electoral district)">Beaver River</a>, where Reform candidate <a href="/wiki/Deborah_Grey" title="Deborah Grey">Deborah Grey</a> defeated Progressive Conservative candidate <a href="/wiki/Dave_Broda" title="Dave Broda">Dave Broda</a> by a nearly 20 percent margin. This was the first sign that Mulroney's coalition was fracturing; the PCs had dominated Alberta's federal politics since the <a href="/wiki/1958_Canadian_federal_election" title="1958 Canadian federal election">1958 election</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-148" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-148">&#91;143&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-149" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-149">&#91;144&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In June 1991, the pro-Quebec sovereigntist <a href="/wiki/Bloc_Qu%C3%A9b%C3%A9cois" title="Bloc Québécois">Bloc Québécois</a> was founded by Mulroney's former <a href="/wiki/Minister_of_Environment_and_Climate_Change" title="Minister of Environment and Climate Change">environment minister</a> and <a href="/wiki/Quebec_lieutenant" title="Quebec lieutenant">Quebec lieutenant</a>, <a href="/wiki/Lucien_Bouchard" title="Lucien Bouchard">Lucien Bouchard</a>. The party's foundation was motivated by the collapse of the <a href="/wiki/Meech_Lake_Accord" title="Meech Lake Accord">Meech Lake Accord</a>, which would have benefited Quebec if it had been ratified. The party attracted a few other PC and Liberal members of parliament.<sup id="cite_ref-150" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-150">&#91;145&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Resignation">Resignation</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Brian_Mulroney&amp;action=edit&amp;section=26" title="Edit section: Resignation"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>Widespread public resentment of the goods and services tax, the early 1990s recession, the fracturing of his political coalition, and his lack of results regarding the Quebec situation caused Mulroney's popularity to decline severely during his second term. Mulroney entered 1993 facing <a href="/wiki/1993_Canadian_federal_election" title="1993 Canadian federal election">a statutory general election</a>. By this time, his approval ratings had dipped into the tens, and were at 12 per cent in a 1992 <a href="/wiki/Gallup_poll" class="mw-redirect" title="Gallup poll">Gallup poll</a>, making him the most unpopular prime minister since opinion polling began in Canada in the 1940s.<sup id="cite_ref-151" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-151">&#91;146&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On February 24, 1993, Mulroney announced his intention to resign as prime minister and retire from politics. In his announcement, Mulroney stated, "Whether you agree with our solutions or not, none will accuse us, I think, of having chosen to evade our responsibilities by sidestepping the most controversial issues of our time." Mulroney claimed his resignation was not related to the consensus that he would be heavily defeated by <a href="/wiki/Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien" title="Jean Chrétien">Jean Chrétien</a>'s Liberals if he led the Tories into the next election and rather argued he could defeat the Liberals if he sought another term.<sup id="cite_ref-152" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-152">&#91;147&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-MulroneyLATimes_153-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MulroneyLATimes-153">&#91;148&#93;</a></sup> The last Gallup Poll taken before Mulroney's announcement of resignation showed the PCs' polling numbers had rebounded to 21 per cent.<sup id="cite_ref-154" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-154">&#91;149&#93;</a></sup> In his final days in office, Mulroney conducted a European "farewell" tour using Canadian federal funds.<sup id="cite_ref-155" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-155">&#91;150&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-APNov1993_156-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-APNov1993-156">&#91;151&#93;</a></sup> On <a href="/wiki/1993_Progressive_Conservative_leadership_election" title="1993 Progressive Conservative leadership election">June 13, 1993</a>, Mulroney was replaced as leader of the Progressive Conservatives by Defence Minister <a href="/wiki/Kim_Campbell" title="Kim Campbell">Kim Campbell</a>. On June 25, 1993, Mulroney resigned as prime minister and chose not to run for reelection at the Commons.<sup id="cite_ref-157" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-157">&#91;152&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Aftermath">Aftermath</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Brian_Mulroney&amp;action=edit&amp;section=27" title="Edit section: Aftermath"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<figure typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Canada_1993_Federal_Election_seats.svg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9b/Canada_1993_Federal_Election_seats.svg/300px-Canada_1993_Federal_Election_seats.svg.png" decoding="async" width="300" height="110" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9b/Canada_1993_Federal_Election_seats.svg/450px-Canada_1993_Federal_Election_seats.svg.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9b/Canada_1993_Federal_Election_seats.svg/600px-Canada_1993_Federal_Election_seats.svg.png 2x" data-file-width="673" data-file-height="246" /></a><figcaption>The House of Commons after the 1993 election <link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1126788409"><div class="plainlist"><ul><li>Red: Liberals (177)</li><li>Blue: Bloc Québécois (54)</li><li>Green: Reform (53)</li><li>Orange: New Democratic Party (9)</li><li>Mauve: Progressive Conservatives (2)</li><li>Grey: Independent (1)</li></ul></div></figcaption></figure>
<p>In the October 25, 1993, election, the Progressive Conservative Party was reduced from 156 seats to two seats in the worst defeat ever suffered for a governing party at the federal level in Canada. The Tories were no longer recognized as an official caucus in the House of Commons since the required minimum number of seats for <a href="/wiki/Official_party_status" title="Official party status">official party status</a> is 12. As an example of the antipathy toward Mulroney, his former riding fell to the Bloc by a lopsided margin; the Tory candidate finished a distant third, with only 6,800 votes out of nearly 40,000 cast.<sup id="cite_ref-158" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-158">&#91;153&#93;</a></sup> The more right-wing Reform Party won over <a href="/wiki/Western_alienation" title="Western alienation">alienated Western Canadians</a> and replaced the PCs as the major conservative party in Canada; they won 52 seats and 18.7 percent of the popular vote, which was greater than the PCs (which won 16 percent of the popular vote). The Bloc replaced Mulroney's voting base in Quebec, becoming the <a href="/wiki/Official_Opposition_(Canada)" title="Official Opposition (Canada)">Official opposition</a> (at 54 seats). In the election, Chrétien's Liberals won a strong majority government.  Mulroney claimed he was not responsible for the obliteration of the PCs,and instead blamed Campbell and her relationship with her boyfriend. In <i><a href="/wiki/The_Secret_Mulroney_Tapes" title="The Secret Mulroney Tapes">The Secret Mulroney Tapes</a></i>, it was revealed Mulroney said of Campbell, "Throughout the whole goddam thing she's been screwing around with this Russian guy. The guy was sneaking into hotel rooms and the campaign bus"; he also said it was "the most incompetent campaign I've seen in my life."<sup id="cite_ref-159" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-159">&#91;154&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-MulroneyG&amp;M_160-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MulroneyG&amp;M-160">&#91;155&#93;</a></sup> In the following <a href="/wiki/1997_Canadian_federal_election" title="1997 Canadian federal election">1997</a> and <a href="/wiki/2000_Canadian_federal_election" title="2000 Canadian federal election">2000</a> elections, the Progressive Conservatives would continue being the smallest party in the House of Commons, holding on to fifth-place status though regaining official party status. In 2003, the party merged with Reform's successor, the <a href="/wiki/Canadian_Alliance" title="Canadian Alliance">Canadian Alliance</a>, to create today's <a href="/wiki/Conservative_Party_of_Canada" title="Conservative Party of Canada">Conservative Party of Canada</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-161" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-161">&#91;156&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="After_politics_.281993.E2.80.932024.29"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="After_politics_(1993–2024)">After politics (1993–2024)</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Brian_Mulroney&amp;action=edit&amp;section=28" title="Edit section: After politics (1993–2024)"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>After leaving office, Mulroney served as an international business consultant and was a partner with the law firm <a href="/wiki/Norton_Rose" class="mw-redirect" title="Norton Rose">Norton Rose</a>. He, up until his death in 2024, sat on the board of directors of multiple corporations, including <a href="/wiki/The_Blackstone_Group" class="mw-redirect" title="The Blackstone Group">The Blackstone Group</a>, <a href="/wiki/Barrick_Gold" title="Barrick Gold">Barrick Gold</a>, <a href="/wiki/Quebecor_Inc." class="mw-redirect" title="Quebecor Inc.">Quebecor Inc.</a>, <a href="/wiki/Archer_Daniels_Midland" class="mw-redirect" title="Archer Daniels Midland">Archer Daniels Midland</a>, Cendant Corp. (New York), AOL Latin America, Inc. (New York), Cognicase Inc. (<a href="/wiki/Montreal" title="Montreal">Montreal</a>) and Acreage Holdings, one of the largest vertically integrated cannabis companies in the United States.<sup id="cite_ref-162" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-162">&#91;157&#93;</a></sup> He was a senior counsellor to Hicks, Muse, Tate &amp; Furst, a global private equity fund in <a href="/wiki/Dallas" title="Dallas">Dallas</a>, chairman of Forbes Global (New York), and was a paid consultant and lobbyist for <a href="/wiki/Karl-Heinz_Schreiber" class="mw-redirect" title="Karl-Heinz Schreiber">Karl-Heinz Schreiber</a> beginning in 1993. He was also chairman of various international advisory boards and councils for many international companies, including Power Corp. (Montreal), Bombardier (Montreal), the China International Trust and Investment Corp. (Beijing), <a href="/wiki/J.P._Morgan_Chase_and_Co." class="mw-redirect" title="J.P. Morgan Chase and Co.">J.P. Morgan Chase and Co.</a> (New York), Violy, Byorum and Partners (New York), VS&amp;A Communications Partners (New York), Independent Newspapers (<a href="/wiki/Dublin" title="Dublin">Dublin</a>) and General Enterprise Management Services Limited (<a href="/wiki/British_Virgin_Islands" title="British Virgin Islands">British Virgin Islands</a>).<sup id="cite_ref-163" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-163">&#91;158&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1998, Mulroney was accorded Canada's highest civilian honour when he was made a <a href="/wiki/List_of_Companions_of_the_Order_of_Canada" class="mw-redirect" title="List of Companions of the Order of Canada">Companion</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Order_of_Canada" title="Order of Canada">Order of Canada</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-164" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-164">&#91;159&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Mulroney_Thatcher_and_Gorbachev_at_Reagan%27s_funeral.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/43/Mulroney_Thatcher_and_Gorbachev_at_Reagan%27s_funeral.jpg/220px-Mulroney_Thatcher_and_Gorbachev_at_Reagan%27s_funeral.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="147" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/43/Mulroney_Thatcher_and_Gorbachev_at_Reagan%27s_funeral.jpg/330px-Mulroney_Thatcher_and_Gorbachev_at_Reagan%27s_funeral.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/43/Mulroney_Thatcher_and_Gorbachev_at_Reagan%27s_funeral.jpg/440px-Mulroney_Thatcher_and_Gorbachev_at_Reagan%27s_funeral.jpg 2x" data-file-width="515" data-file-height="343" /></a><figcaption>At the <a href="/wiki/Funeral_of_Ronald_Reagan" class="mw-redirect" title="Funeral of Ronald Reagan">funeral of Ronald Reagan</a> with former Soviet president <a href="/wiki/Mikhail_Gorbachev" title="Mikhail Gorbachev">Mikhail Gorbachev</a>, former Japanese Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Yasuhiro_Nakasone" title="Yasuhiro Nakasone">Yasuhiro Nakasone</a> and former British Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Margaret_Thatcher" title="Margaret Thatcher">Margaret Thatcher</a></figcaption></figure>
<p>In 2003, Mulroney received the <a href="/wiki/Woodrow_Wilson_Awards" title="Woodrow Wilson Awards">Woodrow Wilson Award for Public Service</a> from the <a href="/wiki/Woodrow_Wilson_International_Center_for_Scholars" title="Woodrow Wilson International Center for Scholars">Woodrow Wilson International Center for Scholars</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Smithsonian_Institution" title="Smithsonian Institution">Smithsonian Institution</a> at a ceremony in Montreal.<sup id="cite_ref-woodrow_165-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-woodrow-165">&#91;160&#93;</a></sup> The award was in recognition of his career in politics.<sup id="cite_ref-woodrow_165-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-woodrow-165">&#91;160&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In January 2004, Mulroney delivered a keynote speech in Washington, D.C., celebrating the tenth anniversary of the <a href="/wiki/North_American_Free_Trade_Agreement" title="North American Free Trade Agreement">North American Free Trade Agreement</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-166" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-166">&#91;161&#93;</a></sup> In June 2004, Mulroney presented a eulogy for former U.S. President Ronald Reagan during the <a href="/wiki/Death_and_state_funeral_of_Ronald_Reagan" title="Death and state funeral of Ronald Reagan">latter's state funeral</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-167" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-167">&#91;162&#93;</a></sup> Two years later, at the request of Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Stephen_Harper" title="Stephen Harper">Stephen Harper</a>, Mulroney travelled to Washington, D.C., along with Michael Wilson, Canada's ambassador to the United States, as Canada's representatives at the <a href="/wiki/Death_and_state_funeral_of_Gerald_Ford" title="Death and state funeral of Gerald Ford">state funeral</a> of former president <a href="/wiki/Gerald_Ford" title="Gerald Ford">Gerald Ford</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-168" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-168">&#91;163&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In February 2005, as part of a physical examination, a CT scan revealed two small lumps in one of Mulroney's lungs. In his youth, Mulroney had been a heavy smoker. His doctors performed a biopsy, which ruled out cancer (his surgery is sometimes cited as an example of the dangers of unnecessary testing).<sup id="cite_ref-169" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-169">&#91;164&#93;</a></sup> He recovered well enough to tape a speech for the <a href="/wiki/Conservative_Party_of_Canada" title="Conservative Party of Canada">Conservative Party of Canada</a>'s 2005 Policy Convention in Montreal in March, though he could not attend in person. He later developed <a href="/wiki/Pancreatitis" title="Pancreatitis">pancreatitis</a>, and he remained in hospital for several weeks. It was not until April 19 that his son, Ben Mulroney, announced he was recovering and would soon be released.<sup id="cite_ref-170" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-170">&#91;165&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On September 12, 2005, veteran writer and former Mulroney confidant <a href="/wiki/Peter_C._Newman" title="Peter C. Newman">Peter C. Newman</a> released <i><a href="/wiki/The_Secret_Mulroney_Tapes:_Unguarded_Confessions_of_a_Prime_Minister" class="mw-redirect" title="The Secret Mulroney Tapes: Unguarded Confessions of a Prime Minister">The Secret Mulroney Tapes: Unguarded Confessions of a Prime Minister</a></i>. Based largely on remarks from the former prime minister, which Newman had taped with Mulroney's knowledge, the book set off national controversy. Newman had been given unfettered access to Mulroney for a thorough biography and claims Mulroney did not honour an agreement to allow him access to confidential papers.<sup id="cite_ref-171" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-171">&#91;166&#93;</a></sup> This led Mulroney to respond at the annual Press Gallery Dinner, which is noted for comedic moments, in Ottawa on October 22, 2005. The former prime minister appeared on tape and very formally acknowledged the various dignitaries and audience groups before delivering the shortest speech of the night: "Peter Newman: Go fuck yourself. Thank you, ladies and gentlemen, and good night."<sup id="cite_ref-172" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-172">&#91;167&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 2014, Mulroney became the chairman of <a href="/wiki/Quebecor" title="Quebecor">Quebecor</a> and defused tensions resulting from the continuing influence of former president and CEO <a href="/wiki/Pierre_Karl_P%C3%A9ladeau" title="Pierre Karl Péladeau">Pierre Karl Péladeau</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-173" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-173">&#91;168&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On December 5, 2018, Mulroney presented a eulogy for former U.S. President George H. W. Bush during the <a href="/wiki/Death_and_state_funeral_of_George_H._W._Bush" title="Death and state funeral of George H. W. Bush">latter's state funeral</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-174" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-174">&#91;169&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Airbus.2FSchreiber_affair"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Airbus/Schreiber_affair">Airbus/Schreiber affair</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Brian_Mulroney&amp;action=edit&amp;section=29" title="Edit section: Airbus/Schreiber affair"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Airbus_affair" title="Airbus affair">Airbus affair</a></div>
<p>On September 29, 1995, the Canadian Department of Justice, acting on behalf of the RCMP, sent a Letter of Request to the Swiss Government asking for information related to allegations that Mulroney was involved in a criminal conspiracy to defraud the Government of Canada.<sup id="cite_ref-175" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-175">&#91;170&#93;</a></sup> The investigation pertained to "improper commissions" allegedly paid to German-Canadian businessman <a href="/wiki/Karlheinz_Schreiber" title="Karlheinz Schreiber">Karlheinz Schreiber</a> (or to companies controlled by him), Brian Mulroney and former Newfoundland premier <a href="/wiki/Frank_Moores" title="Frank Moores">Frank Moores</a> in exchange for three government contracts.<sup id="cite_ref-oliph_176-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-oliph-176">&#91;171&#93;</a></sup> These contracts involved the purchase of Airbus Industrie aircraft by Air Canada; the purchase of helicopters by the Canadian Coast Guard from Messerschmitt-Bolkow-Blohm GmbH (MBB) in 1986; and the establishment of a manufacturing plant for Thyssen Light Armoured Vehicles (Bear Head Project) in the province of Nova Scotia, a project which Mulroney as prime minister had cancelled.<sup id="cite_ref-oliph_176-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-oliph-176">&#91;171&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>This Letter of Request (LOR) "and its contents were to be kept confidential" but the letter was leaked to the media.<sup id="cite_ref-177" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-177">&#91;172&#93;</a></sup> As a result, Mulroney launched a $50 million libel lawsuit against the Government of Canada and the RCMP on November 20, 1995.<sup id="cite_ref-178" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-178">&#91;173&#93;</a></sup> On January 5, 1997, Mulroney agreed to an out-of-court settlement of $2.1 million with the Government of Canada and the RCMP.<sup id="cite_ref-179" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-179">&#91;174&#93;</a></sup> The Oliphant Commission Report in 2010 stated that Mulroney accepted $225 000 from Schreiber, and former justice minister <a href="/wiki/Allan_Rock" title="Allan Rock">Allan Rock</a> said he would have used a different litigation strategy in the libel case had he known about these payments.<sup id="cite_ref-CBC_Jun_2010_180-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CBC_Jun_2010-180">&#91;175&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Mulroney did not disclose that he had received money from Schreiber (which Schreiber alleges was a total of $300,000 in instalments of $100,000 in three separate occurrences between 1993 and 1994 given to Mulroney under the code name "Britan" in brown paper envelopes/bags in denominations of $1,000 during secret hotel exchanges). Later, on February 2, 1998, Schreiber met with Mulroney at Mulroney's invitation to the hotel Savoy in Zurich, where, before a spread of smoked salmon and appetizers, Mulroney tried to get Schreiber to verify that nothing could connect Mulroney to the money.<sup id="cite_ref-181" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-181">&#91;176&#93;</a></sup> Later on, when further information was leaked to the public, Mulroney stated that there was nothing wrong with accepting envelopes of $1,000 bills (which he put into safes and safe deposit boxes without reporting tax on it as income for years after).<sup id="cite_ref-182" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-182">&#91;177&#93;</a></sup> The Oliphant Commission, presided over by Justice Jeffrey Oliphant, reported on May 31, 2010, that Mulroney "failed to live up to the standard of conduct that he himself adopted in the 1995 ethics code.  Oliphant J. said he could not accept Mulroney's testimony that his acceptance of $225,000 cash was an error in judgment.  Rather, it was an attempt to hide the transactions.<sup id="cite_ref-183" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-183">&#91;178&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Conservative_Party_of_Canada">Conservative Party of Canada</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Brian_Mulroney&amp;action=edit&amp;section=30" title="Edit section: Conservative Party of Canada"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Mulroney played an influential role by supporting the merger of the <a href="/wiki/Canadian_Alliance" title="Canadian Alliance">Canadian Alliance</a> (successor of the Reform Party) with the Progressive Conservatives to form the <a href="/wiki/Conservative_Party_of_Canada" title="Conservative Party of Canada">Conservative Party of Canada</a>; Mulroney joined the new party upon its formation in 2003.<sup id="cite_ref-merger_184-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-merger-184">&#91;179&#93;</a></sup> This distinguished him from other prominent PC politicians, such as former prime ministers Joe Clark and Kim Campbell, who became <a href="/wiki/Independent_politician" title="Independent politician">independents</a> as they believed that the Conservative Party was too right-wing and drifting toward social conservatism.<sup id="cite_ref-merger_184-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-merger-184">&#91;179&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>According to press reports, Mulroney's membership lapsed in 2006. In early 2009, Mulroney "called a high-ranking person in the party and asked that his name be removed from all party lists" due to his anger at the continued inquiry into his financial affairs,<sup id="cite_ref-185" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-185">&#91;180&#93;</a></sup> although he denies this claim.<sup id="cite_ref-186" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-186">&#91;181&#93;</a></sup> A Mulroney confidant, speaking on condition of anonymity, called the party's claims preposterous. "He's part of the history of this party; you can't rewrite history. If they're worried about branding, then shut the inquiry down. They're the ones who called the inquiry."<sup id="cite_ref-187" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-187">&#91;182&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Months before the <a href="/wiki/2015_Canadian_federal_election" title="2015 Canadian federal election">2015 federal election</a>, Mulroney endorsed Prime Minister Stephen Harper.<sup id="cite_ref-188" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-188">&#91;183&#93;</a></sup> Mulroney campaigned for Conservative Leader <a href="/wiki/Erin_O%27Toole" title="Erin O&#39;Toole">Erin O'Toole</a> in the run-up of the <a href="/wiki/2021_Canadian_election" class="mw-redirect" title="2021 Canadian election">2021 Canadian election</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-189" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-189">&#91;184&#93;</a></sup> Just a month and a bit later, Mulroney criticized him on the matter of <a href="/wiki/COVID-19_vaccination_in_Canada" title="COVID-19 vaccination in Canada">COVID-19 vaccinations</a>, stating that he should show "leadership" and expel unvaccinated MPs from the Conservative caucus.<sup id="cite_ref-190" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-190">&#91;185&#93;</a></sup> In October 2022, Mulroney said he supported the Conservatives' new leader, <a href="/wiki/Pierre_Poilievre" title="Pierre Poilievre">Pierre Poilievre</a>, but urged him to move closer to the <a href="/wiki/Political_centre" class="mw-redirect" title="Political centre">political centre</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-191" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-191">&#91;186&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Other_political_interventions">Other political interventions</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Brian_Mulroney&amp;action=edit&amp;section=31" title="Edit section: Other political interventions"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In 2003, Mulroney criticized the Chrétien government's foreign policy. He expressed his disappointment with the Liberals strengthening relations with China, Russia, and Germany. Instead, he voiced his support for the United States, stating, "I want to stick with my old friends and allies." He also voiced his support for the <a href="/wiki/Iraq_War" title="Iraq War">Iraq War</a> and said Canada would have supported the United States in Iraq if he was still prime minister.<sup id="cite_ref-192" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-192">&#91;187&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In June 2023, Mulroney praised Liberal Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Justin_Trudeau" title="Justin Trudeau">Justin Trudeau</a>'s handling of negotiations of the <a href="/wiki/United_States%E2%80%93Mexico%E2%80%93Canada_Agreement" title="United States–Mexico–Canada Agreement">United States–Mexico–Canada Agreement</a> (USMCA) and Trudeau's handling of the <a href="/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Canada" title="COVID-19 pandemic in Canada">COVID-19 pandemic</a>. Mulroney also criticized former US President <a href="/wiki/Donald_Trump" title="Donald Trump">Donald Trump</a>, saying that he "was out to sabotage Canada."<sup id="cite_ref-193" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-193">&#91;188&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-194" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-194">&#91;189&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Memoir">Memoir</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Brian_Mulroney&amp;action=edit&amp;section=32" title="Edit section: Memoir"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Mulroney-Reismann_Shill.JPG" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b7/Mulroney-Reismann_Shill.JPG/220px-Mulroney-Reismann_Shill.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="167" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b7/Mulroney-Reismann_Shill.JPG/330px-Mulroney-Reismann_Shill.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b7/Mulroney-Reismann_Shill.JPG/440px-Mulroney-Reismann_Shill.JPG 2x" data-file-width="1008" data-file-height="764" /></a><figcaption>Mulroney appears during an interview with <a href="/wiki/Heather_Reisman" title="Heather Reisman">Heather Reisman</a>, speaking about his memoirs.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Mulroney's <i><a href="/wiki/Memoirs:_1939%E2%80%931993" class="mw-redirect" title="Memoirs: 1939–1993">Memoirs: 1939–1993</a></i> was released on September 10, 2007. Mulroney criticizes Pierre Elliot Trudeau for avoiding military service in <a href="/wiki/World_War_II" title="World War II">World War II</a>, and favourably references sources that describe the young Trudeau as holding <a href="/wiki/Anti-Semitic" class="mw-redirect" title="Anti-Semitic">anti-Semitic</a> nationalist views and having an admiration for fascist dictators.<sup id="cite_ref-195" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-195">&#91;190&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-196" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-196">&#91;191&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Tom_Axworthy" title="Tom Axworthy">Tom Axworthy</a>, a prominent Liberal strategist, responded that Trudeau should be judged on his mature views. Historian and former MP and Trudeau biographer <a href="/wiki/John_English_(Canadian_politician)" title="John English (Canadian politician)">John English</a> said, "I don't think it does any good to do this kind of historical ransacking to try to destroy reputations."<sup id="cite_ref-197" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-197">&#91;192&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-198" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-198">&#91;193&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Death_and_state_funeral">Death and state funeral</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Brian_Mulroney&amp;action=edit&amp;section=33" title="Edit section: Death and state funeral"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Death_and_state_funeral_of_Brian_Mulroney" title="Death and state funeral of Brian Mulroney">Death and state funeral of Brian Mulroney</a></div>
<p>Mulroney had suffered several years of declining health leading up to his death. He had been hospitalized as the result of a fall at his home in Palm Beach and died on February 29, 2024.<sup id="cite_ref-CP-March-15_199-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CP-March-15-199">&#91;194&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-200" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-200">&#91;195&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Mulroney's remains were repatriated on March 8, 2024, at <a href="/wiki/Ottawa_Macdonald%E2%80%93Cartier_International_Airport" title="Ottawa Macdonald–Cartier International Airport">Ottawa Macdonald–Cartier International Airport</a>. A tribute in the House of Commons of Canada was held on March 18, after MPs agreed to suspend sitting.<sup id="cite_ref-201" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-201">&#91;196&#93;</a></sup> Mulroney lay in state at the <a href="/wiki/Sir_John_A._Macdonald_Building" title="Sir John A. Macdonald Building">Sir John A. Macdonald Building</a> in Ottawa on March 19–20, before laying in repose at <a href="/wiki/St._Patrick%27s_Basilica,_Montreal" title="St. Patrick&#39;s Basilica, Montreal">St. Patrick's Basilica, Montreal</a> on March 21–22. A state funeral was held at the <a href="/wiki/Notre-Dame_Basilica_(Montreal)" title="Notre-Dame Basilica (Montreal)">Notre-Dame Basilica</a> on March 23 and was attended by Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Justin_Trudeau" title="Justin Trudeau">Justin Trudeau</a> as well as former prime ministers <a href="/wiki/Jean_Chr%C3%A9tien" title="Jean Chrétien">Jean Chrétien</a>, <a href="/wiki/Joe_Clark" title="Joe Clark">Joe Clark</a>, <a href="/wiki/Stephen_Harper" title="Stephen Harper">Stephen Harper</a> and <a href="/wiki/Kim_Campbell" title="Kim Campbell">Kim Campbell</a>, hockey player <a href="/wiki/Wayne_Gretzky" title="Wayne Gretzky">Wayne Gretzky</a>, former UK Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/John_Major" title="John Major">John Major</a>, <a href="/wiki/Sarah,_Duchess_of_York" title="Sarah, Duchess of York">Sarah, Duchess of York</a>, and actor <a href="/wiki/Ryan_Reynolds" title="Ryan Reynolds">Ryan Reynolds</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-202" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-202">&#91;197&#93;</a></sup> He was buried at <a href="/wiki/Notre_Dame_des_Neiges_Cemetery" title="Notre Dame des Neiges Cemetery">Notre Dame des Neiges Cemetery</a> in Montreal.<sup id="cite_ref-203" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-203">&#91;198&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Legacy">Legacy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Brian_Mulroney&amp;action=edit&amp;section=34" title="Edit section: Legacy"><span>edit</span></a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Mulroney's legacy as prime minister is considered to be mixed. Mulroney made the case that his once-radical policies on the economy and free trade were not reversed by subsequent governments and regarded this as vindication.<sup id="cite_ref-Newman,_p._361_204-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Newman,_p._361-204">&#91;199&#93;</a></sup> His deputy prime minister, <a href="/wiki/Don_Mazankowski" title="Don Mazankowski">Don Mazankowski</a>, said that his greatest accomplishment would be seen as "dragging Canada kicking and screaming into the 21st century". His legacy in Canada is associated mostly with the 1989 Free Trade Agreement<sup id="cite_ref-autogenerated1_130-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-autogenerated1-130">&#91;126&#93;</a></sup> and the <a href="/wiki/Goods_and_Services_Tax_(Canada)" class="mw-redirect" title="Goods and Services Tax (Canada)">Goods and Services Tax</a> (GST). Mulroney argued his government's economic policies helped the subsequent government eliminate the deficit.<sup id="cite_ref-205" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-205">&#91;200&#93;</a></sup> During his announcement of his intention to resign as prime minister, Mulroney responded to criticism of his policies: "I tried to do what I thought would be right for Canada in the long term, not what I thought would be politically popular in the short term."<sup id="cite_ref-MulroneyLATimes_153-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MulroneyLATimes-153">&#91;148&#93;</a></sup> In <i><a href="/wiki/The_Secret_Mulroney_Tapes" title="The Secret Mulroney Tapes">The Secret Mulroney Tapes</a></i>, it was revealed he said of his accomplishments: "You cannot name a Canadian prime minister who has done as many significant things as I did, because there are none."<sup id="cite_ref-MulroneyG&amp;M_160-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MulroneyG&amp;M-160">&#91;155&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Mulroney's intense unpopularity at the time of his resignation led many Conservative politicians to distance themselves from him for some years. His government had flirted with 10 percent approval ratings in the early 1990s when Mulroney's honesty and intentions were frequently questioned in the media, by Canadians in general and by his political colleagues.<sup id="cite_ref-206" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-206">&#91;201&#93;</a></sup> In the 1993 election, the Progressive Conservative Party was reduced to two seats, which was seen as partially due to a backlash against Mulroney, as well as due to the fracturing of his "Grand Coalition." In the 1993 election, nearly all of the Tories' Western support shifted to Reform, which replaced the PCs as the major right-wing force in Canada. The Tories only won two seats west of Quebec in the next decade and kept remaining in fifth (last) place. The Canadian right was not reunited until the PCs merged with Reform's successor, the Canadian Alliance, in December 2003 to form the new Conservative Party of Canada.<sup id="cite_ref-207" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-207">&#91;202&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In her memoirs, <i><a href="/wiki/Time_and_Chance_(Kim_Campbell)" class="mw-redirect" title="Time and Chance (Kim Campbell)">Time and Chance</a></i>, and in her response in the <i><a href="/wiki/National_Post" title="National Post">National Post</a></i> to <i>The Secret Mulroney Tapes</i>, Kim Campbell said that Mulroney left her with almost no time to salvage the Tories' reputation once the bounce from the leadership convention wore off. Campbell claimed Mulroney knew the Tories would be defeated regardless of who led them into the election and wanted a "scapegoat who would bear the burden of his unpopularity" rather than a true successor. In a 2019 interview with <i><a href="/wiki/Maclean%27s" title="Maclean&#39;s">Maclean's</a></i>, Campbell described Mulroney as "a pragmatist, not an ideologue."<sup id="cite_ref-208" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-208">&#91;203&#93;</a></sup> Former <a href="/wiki/Ontario_Premier" class="mw-redirect" title="Ontario Premier">Ontario Premier</a> <a href="/wiki/David_Peterson" title="David Peterson">David Peterson</a>, who supported both of Mulroney's attempts at constitutional reform while premier, told journalist <a href="/wiki/Peter_C._Newman" title="Peter C. Newman">Peter C. Newman</a> of Mulroney, "I would never trust or respect him. He is a pathological liar. In fairness, I don't believe he knows he's lying&#160;... Oh God, you couldn't take anything he said at face value. His essential Achilles heel is his baloney."<sup id="cite_ref-MulroneyG&amp;M_160-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-MulroneyG&amp;M-160">&#91;155&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 2006, Mulroney was named the "greenest" Prime Minister in Canadian history by a 12-member panel at an event organized by <i><a href="/wiki/Corporate_Knights" title="Corporate Knights">Corporate Knights</a></i> magazine.<sup id="cite_ref-209" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-209">&#91;204&#93;</a></sup> Military historians Norman Hillmer and <a href="/wiki/J._L._Granatstein" title="J. L. Granatstein">J. L. Granatstein</a> ranked Mulroney eighth out of 20 among Canada's prime ministers in their 1999 book <i>Prime Ministers: Ranking Canada's Leaders</i>.
